SWIFT Debuts Own XRP-Inclusive Ledger With Dozen Banks

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Home » Ripple (XRP) » SWIFT Debuts Own XRP-Inclusive Ledger With Dozen Banks

Ripple’s XRP extends connections to multiple banks in SWIFT’s blockchain-adopting line-up.

The biggest European financial conglomerate’s grandiose move to release their own blockchain ledger didn’t go unnoticed. This November as planned, the Society for Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunication (SWIFT) debuted the pilot version with 12 banks, some of which are familiar names in the XRP holder community.

One of them, HSBC, is slated to start blockchain-powered operations. HSBC connects its own Tokenised Deposit Service (TDS) to SWIFT’s ledger. The major British bank actively employs Metaco’s Harmonize platform for institutional digital-asset custody (Metaco was acquired by Ripple in 2023). The enterprise-level acquisition strengthened that bond.

5 OF SWIFT'S 17 LEDGER BANKS ALREADY WORK WITH RIPPLE 🚨Swift's new blockchain ledger is live with 17 banks piloting 24/7 cross-border payments.Look who's on that list:🏦 HSBC🏦 Standard Chartered🏦 DBS🏦 FirstRand🏦 BNY, which holds RLUSD's reserves and now holds XRP… https://t.co/z7UOp1KBYz pic.twitter.com/nNP5Ql4vgn

The bank also has historical references to XRPL capabilities in internal materials and some past explorations, with an agenda to expand blockchain-powered business operations. Conducting business operations on Distributed Ledger Technology (DLT) based chains like Ripple’s XRP Ledger serves an advantage of instant settlement & cost-effectiveness.

Aside from them, there’s a few more in the 12-long list. Standard Chartered, another popular British bank, has been running the XRP-related tech stack since 2016. During October 2026 the traditional finance behemoth launched their institutional custody for Ripple’s RLUSD stablecoin on the XRP Ledger through their Dubai (DIFC) branch.

Also, there’s DBS and BNY (BNY Mellon). These two Ripple-tied major banks serve completely different purposes. BNY Mellon is the primary reserve custodian for RLUSD. Meanwhile, DBS is partnered with Franklin Templeton to list a tokenized money-market fund (sgBENJI) on XRP Ledger, tradeable against RLUSD on their digital exchange.

🚨 ES OFICIALSWIFT acaba de confirmar transacciones en vivo en blockchain con más de 12 bancos.Y los reportes dicen que está impulsado por $XRP.Este es el tipo de noticia que la gente lleva años esperando.El sistema antiguo finalmente está siendo reemplazado en tiempo real?… pic.twitter.com/2uJi0VGPI9

This is a distinct product from the SWIFT project. SWIFT’s blockchain ledger is compatible with multiple DLT networks, but Ripple’s XRP Ledger has a crystal-clear advantage in regulatory compliance and native traits. XRP, along with Stellar’s XLM & Hedera’s HBAR has native ISO 20022 support, the new gold standard in financial messaging.

The cross-border payments market, worth over $155 trillion in processed volume annually, powers global trade, remittances & commerce. Yet merchants admit it remains slow, costly and not too efficient when compared to blockchain-based counterparts. 70% of merchants expect all transactions to settle immediately by 2027, which aligns with SWIFT’s vision.

How much of that multi-trillion pie Ripple’s XRP can take is yet to be determined. On the SWIFT topic, Ripple’s CEO Brad Garlinghouse predicted a 14% capture. However, Ripple’s business ventures don’t always translate into blockchain adoption, as seen in the 2025 example. Ripple Prime, the custody arm, processed over $13 trillion without the help of crypto.

Former Premier League manager Neil Warnock believes Manchester United should have looked to sign Ezri Konsa before his £55million move to Arsenal.

Conversely, it has been a miserable start to the season for United, who have not tasted victory in the league since a 5-2 win over Ipswich Town in late August.

‘They spend money on forwards, they spend money on midfield players like you’ve never seen and yet they still haven’t got a defence,’ Warnock told The Mirror.

‘I don’t understand how a top club like that can’t buy defenders – and they’ve never had any full backs that can defend either.

‘They could have bought Konsa this year. Why didn’t they spend money like that? They’d rather spent £100million on a midfielder – even though the defenders can’t defend!’

Arsenal transfer interest faces major obstacle as Santos secure £83m teenage talent

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Arsenal may have to rethink their interest in Santos forward Ignacio Pereira after the Brazilian club secured the teenager’s future with a substantial release clause.

The 16-year-old has caught the eye of a number of European clubs after an impressive campaign in Santos’ youth teams. The likes of Arsenal are said to have been watching his progress.

However, Santos have acted swiftly to secure the services of one of their most exciting young talents. Pereira signed his first professional contract with the club on November 4, tying him to the club until December 2029.

The deal also includes a 100 million euro release clause (around £83.4 million), according to Brazilian journalist Bruno Lima from UOL.

This poses a major problem for Arsenal if they are looking to sign the Argentine but also offers a lot of security for Santos, as they want to develop him as a first-team player.

Pereira has signed a new deal amid increasing interest in his development. Reports in Brazil have previously said that Arsenal, Real Betis, AC Milan, Lille, Standard Liège and Zenit are all keeping an eye on the rising star.

The club have now secured his services for another three years. The new agreement was awaiting registration with the Brazilian Football Confederation when the details emerged.

Pereira has spent three years at Santos, having joined from Juventude in 2023. He previously developed at Grêmio before moving to the São Paulo club.

Pereira has certainly lived up to the expectations. He scored an impressive 6 goals in 17 games in the Brazilian Under-17 Championship, making him the second-highest scorer for Santos. He also scored 4 goals in the Paulista Under-17 Championship.

This is very promising. It is his first season playing at under-17 level.

Pereira has been compared to Julián Álvarez due to his mobility and ability to play outside the box.

But the youngster cites another big name up front as his main inspiration. According to UOL, he cites Manchester City’s Erling Haaland as his inspiration, while Lionel Messi is still his football idol.

His profile suggests a versatile number nine who can play lower down the pitch, be an attacking midfielder and play with another striker. He has impressed the youth coaches at Santos with his finishing ability, physicality and desire to win.

He has a long way to go. Reports from Brazil suggest that Santos want to improve his tactical awareness and behaviour before making him more responsible.

He is being considered by the club for inclusion in their squad for the 2027 Copa São Paulo de Futebol Júnior (known as Copinha) and could start on the bench.

This interest from Arsenal does not necessarily mean that there is an imminent move for Pereira. The player is only 16, and a potential deal is made much more complicated due to a new contract.

Maybe the best course of action for Mikel Arteta’s recruitment team is to keep an eye on him. Santos has made it clear where they stand, and Pereira can now work towards his dream. Being in their first team.

‘Ferritin Face’: What Low Iron Actually Does (and Doesn't Do) to Your Appearance

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Social media influencers claim that having a “ferritin face” may indicate you’re low in iron.

Ferritin is a protein that helps your body store iron. You need iron in order to make healthy red blood cells, which carry oxygen from your lungs to the rest of your body. Iron is also important for bone marrow, organs, healthy muscles, and brain development.

As “ferritin face” makes its way across social media platforms, users are describing several tell-tale signs, including:

Users have also shared images of their face before and after taking iron supplements or getting an iron infusion. Videos about “ferritin face” have garnered millions of views on various platforms.

However, medical professionals state that your appearance alone is not enough to diagnose iron deficiency.

“It’s important to understand that ferritin face is not a medical diagnosis, nor is it a confirmed symptom of iron deficiency. While some people with iron deficiency can appear pale due to low hemoglobin levels in their red blood cells, there are many other symptoms to consider,” said Daniel Atkinson, a general practitioner in the United Kingdom and clinical lead at Treated.

“Notably, these symptoms are also present in many different conditions, and their presence does not necessarily mean iron deficiency anemia or even low ferritin levels. Similar features are seen in people with iron processing and absorption issues, such as gluten intolerance, or in urgent sudden blood loss scenarios,” added Jonathan Jennings, MD, board certified internist at Medical Offices of Manhattan.

Iron deficiency can affect your skin and appearance. These signs are often subtle, but may include:

In some ways, the social media videos are correct. Your skin and face can change when your iron levels are low.

“The facial signs people point to, like pale skin and a washed-out look, mostly come from anaemia, when your haemoglobin has dropped. But iron deficiency and anaemia aren’t the same thing,” said Thom Phillips, a general practitioner in the United Kingdom and Chief Medical Officer at Forth. “By the time low iron shows on your face, your stores are usually close to empty.”

It is important to speak with a healthcare professional, who will run blood tests such as a complete blood count and ferritin levels.

It is also important not to take iron supplements without first checking with your healthcare professional, because having too much iron in your body can also cause negative effects.

Too little and too much iron could raise your risk of infection, which is why it’s important not to take supplements without medical supervision.

The signs and symptoms of iron deficiency can depend on whether you are anemic or not. Some of the early signs may include:

“These symptoms typically occur because your body produces fewer red blood cells, which results in less oxygen being delivered throughout your body, making you feel tired and weak,” said Atkinson.

While your facial appearance can be a sign that something isn’t right, healthcare professionals consider other symptoms and how you feel overall.

They also rely on blood tests to confirm the diagnosis and guide treatment.

“The misinformation is the idea that you can spot it yourself. That leads to two problems: people with low iron who look fine assume they’re OK, and people who look tired start taking iron supplements they don’t need,” said Phillips.

Atkinson also noted that factors such as different settings and environmental conditions can affect how your face appears.

That being said, the “ferritin face” trend may help bring awareness to certain symptoms and when to get them checked.

“[This] trend may raise some awareness about iron deficiency anemia and how low iron levels can impact daily life,” said Atkinson.

“Fatigue and lightheadedness are common side effects of low iron, but it manifests in other ways too, such as paler skin or increased hair shedding, which can be concerning for many people. If you experience these symptoms, you should let your doctor know so they can help treat the condition appropriately,” he said.

This Totally Free App Can Free Up Space on Your Mac

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Your Mac's hard drive is full ... again. What's taking up all that space? Endless applications promise to answer this question and help you free up space, most with a subscription. I've tested many over the years, and few are easier to recommend than Purge.

This totally free, open source app gives you a quick breakdown of what's taking up space on your hard drive and offers a few specific ways to free it up. Even better: There are no ads or upsells. Purge can even delete Apple Intelligence's offline models from macOS 27, which could free up over 10 GB.

But even if you want to keep Apple's AI features, you can still clear up plenty of space. Your Mac, like any computer, tends to build up clutter over time. Sure, you might have forgotten about the games, images, and videos you downloaded, but caches also take up space. These are files downloaded and saved to speed things up, which can be useful. The problem is they can stick around longer than necessary and take up space. And then there are caches and settings left behind by apps you uninstalled ages ago—they're still there, eating up precious bytes.

Purge is designed to help you clean all that out, but it can do more. It's built around lists of files known to be safe to purge. There's also a list of things it will never touch, including crucial system files and your own documents. Finally, Purge sends files to the Trash instead of deleting them outright, so you can always recover them if something breaks.

To get started, download Purge and install it. Open the application, and it will show you what's taking up space on your Mac.

If you want, click the Clean Safe Items button, and the app will handle it, assuming you trust it. I recommend paying a bit more attention, though. First, delete some caches. You'll see a list of caches, all of which are safe to purge periodically.

You'll also see Apple's Aerial screen savers here. These videos are very cool, but take up a lot of space—delete them if you, like most 21st-century people, don't use a screen saver.

There's also a Dev Tools section, which isn't relevant for most people. If you use Homebrew, though, don't skip this—it's an easy way to delete cached installation files you don't need anymore.

The next tool shows you only the largest files on your computer, likely videos or other large media. I ended up moving a few things I'd forgotten about off my laptop—I bet you'll find something, too.

Up next is a tool for uninstalling apps. Note that this doesn't just remove the application—it also removes every file associated with it, including your settings, caches, and anything the application downloaded. Deleting applications in Finder doesn't do this, meaning you might leave all kinds of files behind.

Speaking of that, there's also a tool that shows leftover data from applications you've already deleted. In my case, it was several gigabytes, all basically wasted. I highly recommend checking this section and deleting anything large that you're confident you won't use again.

Finally, so long as we're talking about things you're not using, there's Apple Intelligence. Apple's most recent operating systems come preloaded with offline AI models, which power things like notification summaries, writing suggestions, and the new AI-powered Siri. You might like and use these features, but if not, Apple removed the toggle in macOS 27 that lets you disable them altogether.

That's why Purge has one final tool that can completely remove the Apple Intelligence models. Click it, and you'll see a list of installed models above a delete button. Click the button, and Purge will delete the models and prevent macOS from reinstalling them.

These features are why I plan on keeping Purge around. It's a great way to see what's taking up space on your drive and then do something about it.

US sanctions Canadian money-exchange firms over Iran ties

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

US also offers as much as $20 million and possible relocation for information about Iran’s nuclear program.

The US Treasury imposed financial sanctions on eight Canadian money-service companies it claims have done business with sanctioned ‌Iranian entities.

These companies facilitated the exchange of Iranian rials and other currencies for digital assets, enabling international transactions, a US Treasury Department statement said on Friday.

The sanctions, which serve to isolate the companies from the dollar-based financial system, are the latest in a series of Treasury moves against firms outside the US.

These businesses have facilitated transactions with sanctioned Iranian digital asset exchanges, as well as with digital asset addresses associated with Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC), according to the Treasury.

Related: US plans to seize $1B in crypto linked to Iran this week: Scott Bessent

“Canada cannot be a safe haven for illicit financial facilitators that provide sanctions evasion services to the Iranian regime and its proxies,” said Secretary of the Treasury Scott Bessent. “Treasury will pursue and disrupt the Iranian regime’s financial channels anywhere in the world.”

Cointelegraph reported in August that the Treasury Department’s Office of Foreign Assets Control, or OFAC, was targeting crypto exchanges facilitating the transfer of funds to Iran’s IRGC. Bessent said at the time that the US planned to “increase the economic pressure” on Iran, focusing on financial networks funding the country’s regime “in dollars, rials, or crypto.”

Friday’s designations are a continuation of Treasury’s campaign to “disrupt the regime’s exploitation of digital assets to fund terrorism, human rights abuses, and attacks on US personnel and allies,” the statement said.

It added that the actions were coordinated with FINTRAC, Canada’s financial intelligence unit.

Some of the entities subject to this action have changed their names and addresses to evade detection, the statement noted.

In a separate action on Friday, the US Rewards for Justice program offered as much as $20 million and possible relocation for information about Iran’s nuclear program, including its facilities, equipment and personnel.

Iran’s lack of compliance with the International Atomic Energy Agency on safeguards has led to the agency’s loss of “continuity of knowledge” regarding Iran’s production of enriched uranium and components like centrifuges, the statement said.

US charges three men over $129m scheme that faked CEOs’ voices with AI

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Prosecutors in New York say the men posed as executives and lawyers on calls, then laundered the money through crypto.

The men are Jacob Soall, 41, Sharon Hazan, 54, and Guy Sheetrit, 42. Spanish police arrested Soall and Hazan in June and August, and UK police arrested Sheetrit in September. The US has asked for all three to be extradited.

The scheme ran from 2024 to 2026, according to the indictment. The men would call an employee while posing as the company’s chief executive, using AI to mimic the boss’s voice or image. They would ask for help with a secret deal.

The fake CEO then told the employee to contact a lawyer at a large international law firm. Another member of the group posed as that lawyer and gave the bank details for a large transfer.

The victims included a Missouri hospital system, which lost about $4.8m, and a UK fintech company, which lost about $11.6m. A Rhode Island bank, a California hospital system, a Massachusetts mutual fund, and a Florida pet food company were also hit.

The money went to accounts in countries including Spain, Greece, the UAE, Singapore, and China. It was then turned into cryptocurrency to hide where it came from, prosecutors said.

Soall mainly posed as UK lawyers and kept a handwritten script called Lawyer Call, according to the indictment. Hazan and Sheetrit are accused of laundering the money. Hazan was convicted of bank fraud conspiracy in the same court in 2008.

The three face charges including wire fraud, money laundering, and aggravated identity theft. The most serious counts carry up to 20 years in prison. The charges are allegations, and the men are presumed innocent.

The case is a larger version of a 2019 voice-cloning fraud, in which criminals faked a CEO’s voice to steal $243,000. Since then, AI impersonation scams have grown fast.

HYPE loses $80 support as team distributions, institutional selling weigh on price — Details

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Hyperliquid [HYPE] fell below the $80-mark, a week after reclaiming the level as support. In fact, institutions are continuing to sell the altcoin too. Even though the team has been countering the selling pressure through token burns.

In the last 24 hours alone, HyperLabs distributed over 179K HYPE worth about $14.50 million to six team members’ wallets. The distribution was uneven, with these tokens unstaked about two days ago. These wallets are the ones to watch for potential selling pressure.

However, the protocol’s burn mechanism provided a counterweight to the new supply hitting the markets. Over 21K HYPE, worth about $1.64 million, was bought and burned at an average price of $77.93.

Meanwhile, a subsidiary of Hyperliquid Strategies Inc. (HSI) filed an application with the U.S Commodities Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) to become a Futures commission merchant. At the time of writing, the filing was still awaiting approval.

If approved, U.S clients could trade Perpetual Futures on a regulated platform, though there will be no definitive timing just yet. The HSI, meanwhile, reported $620.6 million in profits for the third quarter of this year. An approval would increase these profits in the next few sessions.

On the price charts, HYPE entered a downtrend, even though it was still confined in a sideways range. After Paradigm’s unstaking, the altcoin fell to around $80 and went on to break down the support. It established a low of $76 before entering a short pullback to the upside.

At press time, HYPE was trading towards the broken support at $80, potentially for a retest. Technically, it suggested that bears were in control, with the CMF showing capital flight as its reading stood at -0.19.

This reading was reinforced by the Directional Movement Index (DMI), with its three main lines all pointing south.

In short, HYPE has been facing conflicting signals lately.

Institutions have been unstaking despite looming regulatory clarity. Therefore, HYPE could hold above $75, a support level that has been in place for the last two months if bullish signals prevail.

Otherwise, if the level breaks down, it would set a precedent for demand zones at $70, $60, or lower.

British soldier ignored order to pull his SIM card, then got blackmailed

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Soldier told repeatedly to lock down devices before deploying, got sextorted, court-martialed.

An Estonian newspaper recently revealed that a British soldier disobeyed a direct order to remove the SIM card from his cellphone before being deployed to a part of Estonia, approximately 90 miles west of the Russian border.

As a result of his refusal to engage in this element of operational security (opsec), the soldier, Daniel Barton-Kennedy, had his phone hacked, and nude photos of himself were stolen and used as blackmail against him.

Eventually, Barton-Kennedy was court-martialed for disobeying a lawful command, pled guilty, was reduced in rank, and ordered to serve 10 months in detention.

According to a 21-page British appeals court document shared with Ars Technica by Eesti Ekspress, the Estonian newspaper that first reported on this saga, the story begins in January 2026.

At the time, Barton-Kennedy was a corporal, with over seven years of service, serving in the 1st Battalion of the Duke of Lancaster Regiment of the British Army.

His unit was deployed to participate in “Exercise Eastern Warrior” in Tapa, Estonia, roughly halfway between Tallinn, the capital city, and the Estonia-Russia border.

While in the United Kingdom, on January 7, Barton-Kennedy, along with other members of his unit, were briefed on their orders and given specific instructions to “remove SIM cards and switch off location settings and to use a trusted virtual private network on all personal electronic devices.”

Once the unit arrived in Estonia, days later, they were given further briefings and reminders of this policy. Despite this, Barton-Kennedy ignored the instruction.

On January 19, Barton-Kennedy informed his platoon commander that he had received a message “from an unknown party who had acquired nude photographs of him from the photo section of his mobile phone and had identified him on social media.”

Barton-Kennedy initially acquiesced to paying the attacker 100 British pounds ($133) but balked when he was asked to pay 250 British pounds ($333), and only then informed his platoon commander what had happened.

Eventually, Barton-Kennedy was court-martialed and pled guilty at a hearing on July 30.

During that process, according to the court filing, Barton-Kennedy understood the orders that were given to him at the time, but asked the court to “consider the personal circumstances” that he faced at the time. Without his phone, he told the court, he could not reach his daughter on her birthday, nor his pregnant partner.

In a written statement, Barton-Kennedy said he was stunned when he received the first blackmail message.

“Despite still being in a state of shock and uncertainty, I recognised that I had a duty to report the compromise without any further delay,” he wrote.

Additionally, he said that he “deeply regrets” the incident and added that serving in the British Army “has been one of the greatest privileges of my life.”

Barton-Kennedy appealed the sentence, which was denied in August 2026.

In response to an inquiry for comment, Lt. Col. EP Mackie, a British Army spokesperson, told Ars in a statement that the security measures imposed are meant to protect the safety of military personnel.

“Any breaches of Army security measures that might compromise personal safeguarding or operational security will be dealt with swiftly, and offending personnel can expect uncompromising disciplinary action,” he wrote.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

⁠Before You Spend on Gravel Suspension, Try Wider Tubeless Tires for a Simpler, Smoother Ride

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Wider tires can add comfort and traction on chunky gravel without the weight of suspension parts.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. How we test gear.

Just the other day a friend getting into gravel was asking me about suspension parts for his relatively new gravel bike. He was considering different suspension components as a means to gain comfort while he rides—especially on chunkier gravel. He mentioned his frame isn’t compatible with a suspension fork, but was looking at parts like a suspension stem and suspension seatpost. If this sounds like you read this before hitting add to cart on those parts.

When I ran a shop I installed plenty of suspension parts on bikes for people who felt it was the only way they could be more comfortable on the bike. Certain riders fit the bill for a suspension seat post or suspension stem. A few customers had prostate issues and a suspension seat post helped them stay on the bike longer. Others had wrist or hand issues and they benefited from a suspension stem. But even those riders could get additional comfort from a simple change.

After trying all the suspension components I could add to my own bike (as well as trying a full-suspension gravel bike), I have realized that throwing suspension at a gravel bike doesn’t necessarily fix the issue of being more comfortable on the ride. In the shop, customers would ask about installing a suspension fork on a hybrid or gravel bike, but not all frames are even compatible with a suspension fork.

Adding suspension parts to the bike can change the overall feel of the bike, not to mention the weight those parts typically add. When riders are looking for more comfort on their gravel bike the component swap I pick first is simple—wider tires. Here we’ll talk about the benefits of wider tires and why going tubeless gives your ride that extra bit of cush.

Step one of getting more comfortable on your gravel bike is setting up your tires as tubeless if they aren’t already set up that way. Tubeless systems allow you to run your pressure lower compared to running tubes, which in combination with a wider tire offers more comfort. Running tubes with a lower tire pressure leaves you susceptible to pinch flats, but tubeless systems remove the tubes from the equation.

One easy way to check if your tires are set up as tubeless is to loosen the nut at the bottom of your valve and gently press it into the rim. If you press it in and air starts to come out, then tighten the nut back up—congratulations, your tires are set up as tubeless!

If they aren’t tubeless, then it’s time to check compatibility. Check the specs of your bike to see if your rim and tire are tubeless-compatible. If they aren’t you’ll have to explore new wheel and tire options, but if they’re compatible you can convert them to tubeless. Some bikes come stock with tubeless-compatible rims and tires, but don’t come set up tubeless out of the box. Tubeless systems require specific rim tape or rim strips to properly set them up. I’ve seen plenty of bikes come with tubes installed, but with rims taped with tubeless tape. Again, check your individual bike’s specs to see if this includes your bike.

If you need to pull the tubes out of your tubeless-compatible wheels and tires, then you’ll need a few things. First is a good set of tubeless valves. My favorite are Muc-Off Tubeless Valves. They come with a valve core remover tool on the valve cap, come in different lengths depending on your rim depth, and there are a myriad of colors to add a little pop to your bike. You’ll also need tubeless sealant. I’ve tested more sealants than I can remember, but the tried-and-true sealant I go back to is Stan’s Sealant. Tara Seplavy, Bicycling’s Deputy Editor, walks through setting up your tubeless tires here.

A bike rarely comes stock with the widest tire that fits in the bike. For example, the Trek Checkpoint SL5 is equipped with 700x42mm, but can fit up to 700x50mm tires when there are no fenders on the bike. Always check the technical specifications for your specific make and model to see how wide you can go. Over the last few years, bike manufacturers have been pushing the envelope to allow for wider and wider tires on bikes. If you have a previous model-year bike that doesn’t mean you can bank on the current model’s tire clearance fitting your bike. Be sure to check the correct generation for your specific make and model. If you go with a tire wider than the maximum width for your bike, you risk damaging the fork and frame.

On my own gravel rig, I recently bumped from 700x40mm tires to 700x45mm tires and noticed the difference immediately. I stuck with the same tire make and model—the Pirelli Cinturato H. The extra five millimeters of width immediately gave more comfort and traction on chunkier gravel and some local singletrack; even with the same tire tread. Five millimeters might not sound like a lot, but it added significant comfort while putting out rides longer than one hundred miles.

Wider tires are the right choice for many—even if you aren’t pushing your mileage into the triple digits. Recently I bumped up the tire width on my dad’s gravel bike. Adding wider tires to his bike (as well as a slightly thicker handlebar tape) leveled up his comfort level while riding. Whether you are taking your gravel bike on singletrack, chunky gravel, hundred-mile rides, or just want a little more comfort on your local lunch loop, go tubeless and bump up your tire width.

Jason Griffin is Bicycling's Senior Health and Fitness Editor, bringing more than a decade of fitness experience and years in the cycling industry to every story. A former personal trainer and bike shop manager, he's lined up for crits, tackled enduros, but prefers getting lost on multi-day bikepacking adventures. Lately though, his toughest balancing act has been fitting trainer rides between family life and the next adventure.

What Will James Bond’s New Era Actually Look Like?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Let’s pass the time until Amazon finally decides to tell us the identity of the new 007.

It’s hard not to get swept up in the James Bond speculation. Has Callum Turner landed the role, as reports suggest? Does Jack Lowden still have a shot? Or will they just give up, feed a JPEG of Sean Connery into ChatGPT, and see what it pumps out? It’s all a fun guessing game, really. But one factor is arguably more important than the man playing 007: the new media machine behind it all.

Daniel Craig's fifteen-year tenure churned through four directors—Martin Campbell, Marc Forster, Sam Mendes, and Cary Joji Fukunaga—who all operated under the watchful eye of Barbara Broccoli and Michael G. Wilson. Their family had held creative control since the film franchise's inception. That was, until the pair sold their share to Amazon last year. Bezos and co.'s first order of business was to hire Dune director Denis Villeneuve, arguably the franchise's first-ever auteur, alongside Peaky Blinders scribe Steven Knight—and it's their collective vision that will really define this new era.

Villeneuve is, in my mind, the philosopher king of modern blockbuster cinema. On top of the scale, flair and the star-studded casts that he brings to his grandest films, including the Dune trilogy, he can be trusted to pose challenging ethical and political questions. He'll no doubt bring that same lens to a franchise that has tried, with varying degrees of success, to address hot-button issues.

Ian Fleming's original novels, and the early films to an extent, position Bond as a near-unstoppable, ass-kicking international superspy serving "Queen and country" with little complaint; a wish-fulfilling antidote to the slow but terminal decline of the British Empire.

On-screen, that approach started to shift in the 1990s. Pierce Brosnan's smirking spy is mocked as a relic of the Cold War. Daniel Craig's grittier films took that further, reckoning with the character's increasingly outmoded presence in a culture that had moved beyond sexist wisecracks and silly gadgets. There was plenty of flag-waving along the way, but Broccoli's films nonetheless asked questions about Britain's standing on the world stage. They even touched on decolonization, albeit on the surface level.

Bringing a Canadian on board may have raised the eyebrows of those expecting Bond 26 to carry on with that kind of national introspection, especially in this current moment, as the exponential rise of the far-right that's swept across Europe in the past ten years has dug its claws deep into British culture. But Villeneuve's filmography is proof that he's up to that job, if he so chooses. Sicario was a refreshingly thoughtful take on a cartel thriller that allowed him to question the morality, stances, and intervention of the CIA in an international operation. Equally, Dune interrogates a messianic white savior in Paul Atreides and whether simply positioning oneself as a hero is enough to make yourself one.

Amazon's involvement is where this potentially gets complicated. The pressure is on for the tech giant to produce an explosion-heavy, action-packed money-spinning Bond blockbuster with an international, classic silver screen appeal: in other words, to not screw it up and get their money's worth. Will they be as interested in dealing with Britain's post-imperial neurosis as the Broccoli family were? Or will Bezos’s Bond be laser-focused on grander, more universal anxieties (if even that)?

If one person can stop that from happening, it’s Steven Knight. From big-screen projects like Spencer, his take on Princess Diana’s troubled years in the palace, through to series like Peaky Blinders, House of Guinness and SAS Rogue Heroes, questions of national and cultural identity are central to his writing. Plus, as you'd expect, he's a proper Bond fan.

“It's fantastic to be invited to do it—I can't wait to get started,” he told The Times last year. “I'm hoping that, being a Bond fan for so many years, it will be imbued into me and I will be able to produce something that's the same but different, and better, stronger and bolder.”

Hand-in-hand with Villeneuve, we could be in for something special.

Grab your Walkman, we’re going back to the ‘80s.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Sony has revived a cult classic of 80s tech design, and retro fans are foaming at the mouth. Many of us will be familiar with the iconic Walkman, but what was the perfect accompaniment to this newfangled portable music device? Well, the MDR-3 headphones, of course. And now they're back with a bang.

It shouldn't come as a surprise given the rising popularity of wired headphones. While it's only been a decade, we've come full circle, and now they're officially cool again. Sony's MDR-3R USB-C Wired Headphones are going for a tasty £49, but sadly they're currently sold out. But thankfully, if you're on the hunt for something similar, there are several popular dupes.

The new and improved MDR-3R's are all about preserving heritage. From the packaging to the design of the headphones themselves, there's a distinctly retro appeal to the design, but it's what's inside that counts. The open-air design creates a spacious, airy sound, offering natural, clear vocals and well-balanced sound with rich bass and extended highs.

Another retro competitor, the Koss Porta Pros offer elite sound with 60-ohm dynamic drivers, for sound clarity, rich bass, and an ultra-wide frequency response of 15–25,000 Hz. A premium, Koss-branded leatherette pouch offers easy portability thanks to the headphones' collapsible build. With a bolder design than the MDR-3Rs, these are wireless headphones built to stand out, perfect for retro lovers who want a unique, authentic look.

Wired EarPods have been having quite the style resurgence, spotted on celebrities like Bella Hadid, Harry Styles and Zendaya (check out @wireditgirls on Instagram for more). The effortless nonchalance of wired EarPods has made them the unshakable leader in the wired headphone game. Simple, clean and cool, they're a no-brainer if you want a more lowkey look.

I almost died from flu. My life didn't go back to normal after.

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Madi Allen was 12 years old when she almost died from flu. Now 28, she shared her story with Live Science.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In February 2011, Madi Allen, a healthy 12-year-old in Central Illinois, who loved playing sports, almost died from the flu. The influenza B virus resulted in her developing necrotizing pneumonia, where the infection triggers inflammation that destroys the tissue of the lungs, methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus (MRSA), and her kidneys shut down. She was put on life support and dialysis, and was intubated for five weeks. She spent another two months in hospital and a decade recovering from the damage caused.

Allen, now 28, shared her experience with Live Science.

I was 12 years old when it all started. I was just a normal seventh grader, I played soccer and basketball. I didn't really have any health issues beforehand — just sports-induced asthma.

That weekend, I started to feel a little sick, but nothing too bad. My mom kept giving me over-the-counter medicine and it would work for a bit, then once it wore off, it would get really bad. I'd planned to go to one of my friend's birthday parties then spend the night with my best friend. I went to the party, but I texted my mom saying I should come home because I really wasn't feeling well, which was not usual for me at all. That night I asked my mom to sleep with me because I just felt terrible.

That was Saturday. By Sunday night, the only position that I could get comfortable in was my mom holding me like a baby. My mom just held me all weekend. My whole body just hurt and I started feeling like I couldn't breathe properly. That got way worse Monday morning.

We were waiting for my pediatrician to open up and my mom suggested taking a shower, saying, "Maybe that will help you feel a bit better before we go to the doctor." She had to help me take a shower because I was so weak, I couldn't stand by myself. She stepped away for a second just to grab a towel and said that when she came back, my lips were turning gray and my eyes were starting to look sunken in. She knew that something was not right so she called my dad, who was at the grocery store, and told him to just leave everything, that we need to get to the hospital.

Allen was put on life support and had to be intubated for five weeks after getting sick with flu.

Our local hospital is just a small rural hospital — everybody gets transferred out. They diagnosed me with double pneumonia and then sent me to a different hospital 30 minutes away that specialized more in kids.

My dad rode with me in the ambulance while my mom went home and grabbed a few things — she thought we'd just be there for a few days. By the time we got to the second hospital, I was in respiratory distress. They wanted to intubate me straight away but were waiting until my mom got there so she could talk to me — my dad did the best he could, but my dad didn't know how to calm me down.

I don't think the doctors even told me that they were putting me to sleep. My mom had to be the one to tell me. I remember being very scared. They put this mask on my face for the BiPAP [bilevel positive airway pressure machine, which delivers pressurized air to a patient] and I felt like that was suffocating me. It didn't feel like it was helping at all, which, I guess, at that point, it wasn't. They were just trying everything they could. I was begging them to take it off of me, crying and just saying, "one minute, just take it off for one minute."

Finally my mom tried to calm me down and she's like, "They're gonna have to put you to sleep now." I remember her telling me, "You're just gonna go to sleep for like a couple of hours and then you're gonna wake up and you're gonna feel a lot better."

My mom had to leave the room while they were doing that. As she was walking out the door, the last thing I said was "Mom" and then I put my hands in a heart shape. That was the last thing I did and the last time my mom heard my voice for five weeks.

After I was intubated, things got worse. I had to be put on life support so needed to be moved to a bigger hospital in St. Louis, Missouri. The flight team came to evaluate me and they later told me they didn't think they were ever going to get me to Saint Louis. They thought I wasn't going to make the trip. But they said my mom was begging them to help me, everyone in my family was begging them, so they did everything they could to get me on the helicopter. They worked on me for several hours — every time they tried to move me something would go wrong — but eventually they got me there.

The doctors had to call my parents to get permission to do surgery and put me on life support. My mom said that every time she heard my dad's phone ring, she thought they were calling to tell them that I didn't make it. They were told twice that it was more than likely I was going to pass than make it. They were trying to prepare my parents that I wasn't going to make it.

After getting out of hospital, it took years for Allen to recover, eventually having part of her lung removed that had been badly damaged by the virus.

I woke up five weeks later, and it was very confusing. Everybody was told if I asked what day it was, just to tell me the day of the week. When someone accidentally told me the date, I started crying because my aunt was supposed to be getting married a few weeks later and I was worried about being at her wedding. My mom wasn't there, so my dad had to calm me down and then he told me the wedding had been pushed to October so I could be there.

The second thing I was worried about was that I was supposed to take a test on the Constitution and that I'd missed it. I ended up taking it in the hospital. I was there for 93 days. I got out the very last week of school, so I got to go in on the last day of school and surprise my classmates and my teachers.

That summer, I didn't really do anything besides go to doctor's appointments. I was doing physical therapy, occupational therapy and speech therapy — literally all my time was spent at the hospital in therapy. I'd missed three months of school, but I got to start eighth grade with all my friends, and I ended up playing basketball again. But the next several years was a lot of hard work of trying to get back to my normal.

It probably took the rest of junior high and high school to get back to how I would have been before. It was a very long process and during that time I got sick a lot, so I ended up back in the hospital several times. And it was hard going through that as a teenager — I felt like everybody else is living their normal life, and I felt like I missed out on a lot. Looking back now, I still feel like I did miss out a lot. But I think we made the best of the circumstances.

During COVID, when I was 22, I kept getting sick, and the doctors finally told me that part of my lung was so damaged from the flu it was causing infections, so they gave me an option of getting part of my lung removed. It was a major surgery, but I did it and that was probably the best decision I ever made, because it's been five years and I have not been in the hospital once.

I moved to Florida about three years ago, and I started working as a medical assistant in a pulmonary clinic. This semester I got accepted into respiratory therapy school, so for the next two years, I'm going to be in school for respiratory therapy. It's kind of a full circle moment, to come back into the medical field, because there was a point in my life where everybody was asking me, "Oh, do you want to be a doctor? Do you want to be a nurse?" And I'm like, "Absolutely not." I was still so sick that I was there all the time. I never wanted to be there.

I went to college and I got a degree in criminal justice, but I never really knew what I wanted to do. I ended up getting a job at our local hospital — that first hospital that I went to. Every time they put me in the ER I just loved being there. That job made me realize that this is what I wanted to do.

When people hear my story, they don't realize that if I don't tell the end — after the hospital until now — they don't realize that my life didn't go back to normal after. It's taken 10 years. And it's hard. Some of the other family members that we've gotten to know through Families Fighting Flu, it's difficult listening to their stories. Sometimes I feel like I have this survivor's guilt. Their stories sound exactly like mine — but their kids didn't make it.

Before I got sick I got the flu shot every year, but that year we just got busy and it got put on the back burner. Since getting sick I have always made it a priority to get the flu shot every year. I know it is not a guarantee that I will never get the flu again — but hopefully it will reduce the chances of it ever getting that bad again.

With thanks to Families Fighting Flu, a non-profit organization that promotes flu vaccination and raises awareness of the severity of the infectious disease through families that have been affected.

'I think we will prevail': NASA chief Jared Isaacman on Artemis, China and the new moon race (exclusive)

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

"I think the implications are pretty severe, which is why we're taking it so seriously and putting such extreme focus on the mission right now."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman is serious about launching "big rockets" to the moon every single year for the agency's Artemis program. And the new space race with China is fueling that ambition.

"I feel good. I feel like we are certainly on a more achievable path to the lunar surface today than where we were before," Isaacman told Space.com.

We recently sat down with the NASA chief to discuss the state of the Artemis program, and, off the bat, he made it clear that he's confident in the U.S. space program following the success of April's Artemis II mission around the moon. In fact, just prior to our conversation, it was announced that Artemis II commander Reid Wiseman will become the new deputy director of NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory. But the road from Artemis II's success to crewed lunar landings and, later, a permanent moon base will take a lot of launches of NASA's Space Launch System (SLS) megarocket, Isaacman stressed.

"You know, a lot of people, especially in the space community, start getting [into] if the landers are there or not. I don't really care about that right now. What I care about is that we are launching big rockets no less than once a year," he said.

"I think that's imperative for NASA to be doing these deep-space human spaceflight missions right now," Isaacman added. "So, we had a great mission in April of '26. We will launch again in '27."

That coming launch will be for Artemis III, a crewed mission to low Earth orbit that will test docking procedures between NASA's Orion capsule and one or both of the Artemis program's private lunar landers — SpaceX's Starship and Blue Origin's Blue Moon.

"We need to continue to exercise those muscles," he said, referring to SLS liftoffs. "And, honestly, once a year isn't great, either. I mean, I don't think we're going to get back to the early [space] shuttle days, you know, four missions a year. I don't think we're going to get back to where we were with Apollo, but once a year is the minimum … I would testify that to Congress in this role years down the road.

"If NASA is going to be doing deep-space missions, complicated missions, [we should] launch at least once a year and get as much out of it as you can for taking the risk and spending the taxpayer dollars and building towards something greater," Isaacman added. "That's what we're doing with Artemis III, and that's what's going to better prepare us for Artemis IV."

So, how is NASA coping with the challenges that face them as they approach Artemis III ahead of a crewed lunar landing on Artemis IV, which is targeted for 2028? While NASA can learn some lessons from past moon missions with Apollo, today it's primarily relying on the extensive experience and talent within the agency, according to Isaacman.

"Artemis II is very different from Apollo 8, and Artemis III will be very different from Apollo 9," he said. "Our approach is not exactly the same as it was … But what is the same right now? The spirit, the energy, the potential within NASA. The talent of the workforce and industry is no different than the 1960s."

There's another similarity to the Apollo era: NASA has a rival in its race to the moon. While the adversary in the 1960s was the Soviet Union, today the U.S. is competing against China.

"It is a real space race," Isaacman said. But, he added, "this one is very different than the first one."

"Everything is closer than it was, not just in space. I mean, we had the edge in computing [and] every one of the technology domains during the Cold War. Our capabilities were better by a mile," he said. "[But with] China, it's not [like that]. They're very close."

"We're saying we're going to land on the moon at the end of 2028. China is saying by the end of 2029," Isaacman added. "And it's like, Well, are they going to pull it off? Yes, they will. They've showcased their landers, their rovers, their suits, two-launch architecture with built-in reusability."

So NASA is taking the Chinese threat very seriously, according to Isaacman.

"The consequence of getting this wrong, I'm telling you — it's going to be felt around the world," he said. "It will call everything into question."

"Should I be using American superintelligence or AI? Should I be doing Chinese? Should I be buying their computers? Should I be [buying] ours? Buying their cars, their airplanes, their ships? Whose security guarantees to trust?" Isaacman added. "I think the implications are pretty severe, which is why we're taking it so seriously and putting such extreme focus on the mission right now. But we have that playbook of success, and I think we will prevail."

Chelsea Gohd served as a Senior Writer for Space.com from 2018 to 2022 before returning in 2026, covering everything from climate change to planetary science and human spaceflight in both articles and on-camera in videos. With a M.S. in Biology, Chelsea has written and worked for institutions including NASA JPL, the American Museum of Natural History, Scientific American, Discover Magazine Blog, Astronomy Magazine, and Live Science. When not writing, editing or filming something space-y, Gohd is writing music and performing as Foxanne, even launching a song to space in 2021 with Inspiration4. You can follow her online @chelsea.gohd and @foxanne.music

Please Don’t Sit on the Flowers

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Long-lost Georgia O’Keeffe landscape goes to auction, Brancusi gets lost at MoMA, interview with Doris Salcedo, and is showing at documenta worth it?

Sitting at September Cafe, I watch the autumn leaves separate from the trees and get carried away gently by the wind. They swirl and twirl, performing a merry little dance in the air before falling to their final resting place.

News has just come in about a sweeping Democratic victory in the midterm elections, as fierce and awesome as a Gustave Courbet wave. But I see nobody popping champagne bottles in the streets. A pregnant black cat with a hanging belly saunters by.

Lying back in the dentist’s chair, a TV screen in front of me shuffles picturesque views from exotic places. They're idyllic to the point of not looking real. My doctor, a Lebanese, shows me an X-ray of my mouth, which looks like Beirut at the height of the Civil War.

The sky over the Robert F. Kennedy Bridge is gray, and the Harlem River is brooding as I make my way to Taryn Simon’s exhibition Father Country I Do Love You at the Guggenheim Museum. The show tells the heroic tales of an FBI agent whose alleged career intersects with major events in American history. Cold, antiseptic photographs encased neatly in plexiglass make up a detective story linking the protagonist to the explosion of TWA Flight 800, the 9/11 attacks, the bombing of the United States embassy in Nairobi, Bill Clinton’s impeachment, and more. Trudging up the Guggenheim’s spiraling galleries, I wonder whether this patriotic show is organized and sponsored by the US State Department. Simon makes you read a lot, but all that reading is just meant to distract you from thinking.

Jodie Foster narrates the audio guide for those who don’t have the patience to read long-winded wall texts. At the cafeteria, while eating a bikini sandwich, I see a woman who looks exactly like Judi Dench, another actress. “I love storytelling,” she tells a friend before plunging her teeth into a chocolate croissant.

On my way out, I once again resent how this museum forces everyone to exit through the gift shop to encourage commerce, but I buy myself a $5 Guggenheim bag anyway. Outside, in one of the manicured gardens of the Upper East Side, a small sign pleads, “Please don’t sit on the flowers.”

I cross the street into Central Park through Engineers’ Gate, climb the steps past a gilded bust of John Purroy Mitchel, NYC mayor between 1914-1918, and look out over the Jacqueline Kennedy Onassis Reservoir. I think of the Kennedys, of Trump, and of Simon. A worker collects brown leaves in plastic bags and tosses them into a truck wrapped in a photo of green leaves. A mother tries to snap a photo of her child, but the unruly kid refuses to pose for the camera. He throws a fit, falls, and, to her shock, ends up sitting on the flowers.

What I run on my $10 USD OpenStick homelab

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

A while ago I bought an OpenStick on a whim for 10 dollars (including shipping!) because it seemed like such a cool novelty piece of hardware.

Now, you might look at this hardware and think âmy watch is more powerful than thatâ, and you wouldnât necessarily be wrong, but thereâs still a lot of fun to be had with a device this size, and itâs a great gadget to get started with self-hosting.

The installation process is a bit involved, but once youâre up and running it runs Debian, which brings all the classic goodies such as systemd (among other things, a scheduler and service monitoring for Linux) and Podman (lightweight containerization). For 10 USD you basically get a home server with minimal power usage and zero noise!

Thereâs actually a lot of useful software that runs great on this thing. Some of my favorites are:

This is a little service that connects to any running Google Cast device and skips sponsorships (and sometimes ads). It barely uses any resources, and after a while you forget that it exists, but it does make the whole YouTube experience a lot nicer!

AdGuard Home blocks bad domains at the DNS level: spyware, malware, and so on. These days devices have a tendency to spy on you. Smart TVs run content recognition continuously, washing machines scan your network, and your coffee maker might be spying on you.

A DNS blocker cannot block all bad requests (youâll need a proper firewall for that), but it sure can block a whole lot. After adding a few blocklists, 60% of my queries are now blocked. Most seem harmless, but Iâm pretty sure that some arenât.

Iâm running my own fork, which can handle large blocklists in an efficient manner, but you probably shouldnât run binaries provided by random strangers, so Iâve also included the link to the official version below (which is totally fine if you limit yourself to smaller blocklists).

AdGuard Home Next-Gen - 40 MB (independent of the size of the blocklist) AdGuard Home - 40 MB (with a small (200k) blocklist)

Zeroclaw is an AI agent harness. It supports 30 channels (Telegram, email, WhatsApp, etc.), has sensible security defaults, a nice web UI, and integrations with all the major LLM providers.

I ran OpenClaw for a while on my Kubernetes cluster, but it mostly caused a lot of frustration: it worked, but it used 1 GB+ of memory, broke with every update, and they kept changing functionality in ways that broke my existing workflows. Zeroclaw feels like a breath of fresh air. It uses ~3 MB RSS when idle, covers all the functionality that I need, and the security defaults are sensible and easy enough to disable. The next version will (hopefully!) have rich message support for Telegram, implemented by yours truly. The PR can be tracked here!

Iâve given Zeroclaw access to the OpenStick (but nothing beyond that). Effectively itâs a root shell, but given that the blast radius is limited, I figured it would be acceptable. I mostly use this to monitor the OpenStick, with questions such as âanything in the logs that requires my attention?â or âhow is the disk space?â. The biggest advantage is that it communicates through Telegram, so if Iâm in the mountains with limited signal, I can still ask it how my little server is doing :)

I looked at alternatives such as Picoclaw and Nullclaw, and no doubt theyâre good as well. However, Zeroclaw seemed to have the best documentation and the most active community, so I went with that :)

Cloudflared makes it possible to expose all these services securely to the big, bad internet, for free, without exposing any ports. The downside might be that the NSA might be able to read everything you do, but for a homelab I consider that to be an acceptable trade-off.

Beszel shows a pretty graph of memory, CPU usage, and so on, and alerts you if something looks suspicious, all while using very little memory.

I ran Grafana and the whole stack on my Kubernetes cluster for a while, and itâs powerful, but not extremely user friendly, and it felt like overkill.

Beszel is a really awesome solution: it looks pretty and requires ~20 MB, 10 MB for the agent and 10 MB for the server. It has built-in alerting for a whole bunch of protocols, and I let it send messages to Telegram when something requires my attention.

Dozzle is a pretty interface for managing Podman containers. It has a large overlap with Beszel, so if I ever need to save 10 or 20 MB, Iâll get rid of one of them. Dozzle has a way better interface for checking Podman logs, but it doesnât offer the same level of stats as Beszel does.

Beszel monitors your server(s). Gatus monitors your services. In other words, if you would like to get a notification when your webserver or database has crashed, youâll want Gatus. It also creates a really pretty web interface!

RIP Google Reader. I tried Feedly for a while, but 7 USD for the Pro version was just a little bit more than I felt comfortable with. Besides, why pay when you can have hours of fun setting up your own alternative?

I tried a whole bunch of RSS readers and eventually settled on Fusion. It does all I need, looks good, feels responsive, and uses 22 MB of RAM.

Browser bookmarks are great, however a lot of my older bookmarks have stopped working because the sites disappeared, the URLs changed, or for a myriad of other reasons. Shiori fixes this by offering a great bookmark manager that also automatically archives the website. It does so in ~10 MB. You can expect some spikes when archiving, but nothing that our mighty OpenStick cannot handle!

Just a little service that sends a message to my Telegram whenever one of my containers has an update available.

Taller Alberto Calleja nestles concrete Casa Nova into tree-filled site on Mexican coast

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Photography by Onnis Luque (unless stated otherwise)

A tree-filled “fissure” bridged by elevated timber walkways cuts through the centre of this house on Mexico’s southern coast, designed by local architecture studio Taller Alerto Calleja.

Named Casa Nova, the holiday home is nestled on a long, narrow site overlooking the sea in Mazunte, Oaxaca, and comprises two skinny trapezoidal volumes and a pair of cubic annexes finished in exposed concrete.

Taller Alberto Calleja (TAC) said the design was informed by the site’s narrow sea frontage, which led to the creation of a narrow, tree-filled “fissure” at the site’s centre that frames views to the sea.

“The main strategy was to create a continuous fissure of vegetation and water at the centre of the site, fragmenting the programme into two longitudinal volumes,” the studio told Dezeen.

“The intermediate void becomes the protagonist of the project. Within it, a reflecting pool functions as a gathering and transitional space, connecting both volumes through elevated bridges,” it added.

Casa Nova is divided across the two trapezoidal volumes, with social spaces including the living, dining and kitchen space to the east and a block of bedrooms to the west.

The flat outer edges of these volumes have been left largely blank, while the angled elevations facing the central trees and forest can be opened up entirely via sliding and folding timber screens, leading onto a network of steps, terraces and walkways that link up the site.

These timber screens feature alternating solid sections and angled louvres, allowing light and air to enter the home even when closed.

“The angles, changes in direction, fragmentation and relationship between solid and void create a dynamic architecture whose perception transforms as one moves through it,” said the studio.

“In this context, exposed concrete and wood in its natural state became a logical choice, not only for their inherent qualities and durability, but for their ability to integrate into the surroundings,” it added.

Prow-like terraces on the roof of both volumes extend southwards to capture sea views and overlook a central swimming pool designed to blend with the horizon.

At the back of the site, two cubic volumes contain bedrooms accompanied by their own social spaces, green courtyards and pools, allowing them to be occupied independently from the main home if desired.

Other projects recently completed by TAC include Casa Mavra, a home in Valle de Bravo that contrasts its forested site with two angular volumes finished in black concrete.

Elsewhere in Mexico, Studio Font recently completed Casa Diez Pies, which steps down a lush coastal cliff, and Dosorozco created Casa Sima, a forest dwelling with a textured material palette.

Photography by Onnis Luque (unless stated otherwise)

Unlocking any car from X brand in most of the world

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Co-founder of Assetnote, security researcher.

I've always had a soft spot for this particular car brand. Our family has owned a few of them over the years, and they've served us incredibly well. In 2023, I bought a new four wheel drive/SUV from them, and it was the first time I had access to their connected car platform.

Having looked at platforms like this before (previous works), I was sceptical about how secure they really were. I suspected there was something there, but life kept getting in the way of actually looking. This July, I collaborated with Sam Curry to take a look at the security of a major automaker's connected car systems. Throughout this post, I'll refer to them simply as "the automaker".

When you typically think of car hacking, it's often easy to jump into the hardware side of things, but my background sits pretty squarely in compromising internet-facing web applications and infrastructure. Regardless of all the hardware bits and bobs, it all connects back to some service that, by design, has to be reachable from the internet.

The journey started with a simple question: what infrastructure does the app talk to when I remotely unlock my car? To work this out, I downloaded the APK and decompiled it, eventually reconstructing the API requests that were being made.

Going through the code, the primary point of contact seemed to be assets under *.redacted-telematics.com.au:

Almost every part of the consumer application called back to redacted-telematics.com.au. What else existed under this domain?

Running connected car infrastructure can be quite complex, and managing it requires a mix of admin tools, staging, dev, and QA environments, to make sure that customers get a clean experience. Whether all of that should be sitting on the public internet, available to anyone willing to do some basic reconnaissance, is a different story.

One asset caught my attention more than the others. admintool.redacted-telematics.com.au returned a page titled TEST HARNESS. Who can resist a subdomain with admintool in it?

Visiting this subdomain presented a very plain and grey login screen, which didn't exactly ooze quality, given it presented a logout button before we'd logged in.

I love that most single-page applications ship their entire JavaScript bundle even when all they need to render is a login page. Sure, some more modern frameworks split out JavaScript files so that they only serve what is necessary, but in this case, we could comb through the large JavaScript bundle to understand what this "admin tool" actually let you do.

Turns out this admin tool let you do quite a lot. It had a rich API that let the automaker's employees interact with customer accounts and production vehicles with very few restrictions. Reconstructing the requests, we found that a lot of these APIs needed no authentication beyond the hardcoded API keys sitting in the JavaScript bundle.

For example, we could look up user accounts by email address:

But that's just not good enough. If we want to unlock any of their cars from just knowing the number plate, we're going to need to do better than that.

In Australia, a number plate gets you the VIN (vehicle identification number) fairly easily through state-based registration lookup sites.

Fortunately, there was an API that let you do exactly that:

Now, how do we send commands to any car we want? Surely this admin tool would let us do that? Unfortunately, no. It let us provision features to a car, add ourselves to a user's account as a secondary user, and even generate remote access codes. But for all that functionality, none of it actually got us remote control of a vehicle.

Sam and I then spent two weeks digging into how the automaker manages their central identity system: the software they use, the way commands are issued, and how consumers authenticate.

When a user goes through the authentication flow for the automaker's mobile app, requests are made to login.redacted-identity.com.au, and after going through an MFA flow, a consumer auth token is finally issued. This auth token is then used for subsequent requests to api.redacted-telematics.com.au (both REST APIs and GraphQL). Once you have that auth token, it's pretty much game over. You can control a user's car (remote unlock/lock, horns, engine, etc).

So if we could mint a token for any user, we'd finally be able to control anyone's connected car. This is the angle we went for.

The automaker is a heavy user of ForgeRock's identity software (OpenAM/OpenIDM). This is the cornerstone of their authentication mechanisms. Pulling the subdomains for *.redacted-identity.com.au, we quickly found their OpenIDM instance at openidm.redacted-identity.com.au. This is the system that manages every identity registered on the automaker's connected car platform.

This all clicked together when we were combing through the JavaScript files under *.redacted-telematics.com.au and we came across an absolutely cursed vendor JavaScript bundle that embedded admin credentials for OpenIDM 😱:

Even though these admin credentials were meant to be for their staging environment, given the hygiene we'd seen so far, we were pretty sure they'd work in production too.

Looking closer at OpenIDM, we couldn't tell whether the automaker knew how fragile it was. One thing was obvious though: they'd invested heavily in WAF rules to block access to the sensitive APIs that ForgeRock typically lets you call via /openidm/*.

Even with the origin IP, any request to /openidm/ came straight back as an Nginx WAF block. Someone had put real effort into hardening these assets (despite them being externally accessible).

Fair enough, and we respect the hardening. But that much WAF investment usually means bandaids over something known to be bad. They probably understood how bad it was to have this asset exposed at all, and some business case kept it there anyway.

I'm not sure a WAF rule has ever stopped us when we're motivated enough, so we invested days into finding a way out of these protections, using our trusty path traversal tricks. How hard could it be?

Validating the admin credentials we found inside the JavaScript bundle was not that tough. The Australian OpenIDM instance had a few paths whitelisted like /XUI/ and /UI/, and path traversal characters were not blocked, so something as simple as /XUI/..%2fopenidm/endpoint seemed to at least partially work:

But it can never be as simple as that, right? The path traversal still didn't get us to the endpoints under /openidm/, thanks to quirks in how OpenIDM handles routing. Every endpoint would error out like so:

In OpenIDM's world, there were no servlets (or resources) registered against paths that included the path traversal within them. So it would just error out, even though it issued us an admin JWT.

We were incredibly annoyed by this. A simple quirk in routing mechanisms (probably not even designed as a security consideration) was blocking the full attack chain from working. We sat frustrated for a few days, and we thought that the path traversal angle just wasn't going to work. We did find a few other endpoints (user upload, for example) that looked like they might work, but we hit another quirk, a servlet path check, that prevented us from using them meaningfully.

All of this made us ask a different question: why expose these OpenIDM instances externally at all? Surely there had to be some legitimate use case for these assets to be exposed on the internet? We went back to the different JavaScript bundles we had collected from their assets and searched them for communications to OpenIDM. We turned to the decompiled Android application and found the following:

These were clearly endpoints within their OpenIDM asset that were contactable from the Android app. So, maybe these were accessible without the WAF getting in the way?

What other custom endpoints existed? With some very light brute forcing and guessing, we quickly found a getUser endpoint. Guessing at parameters, we found it accepted mail, guid, and phone. Combined with our admin credentials, what did this really let us do?

The response came back with the password hash, PIN code hash, mobile push tokens, and PII including mobile numbers and full names. There was no WAF block on this endpoint, so no path traversal was needed and the correct servlet responded.

Don't get me wrong, this was already pretty impactful. But the passwords were hashed with PBKDF2-SHA512, and needing time on a hash cracking rig for every unlock, with no guarantee of success, felt a bit lacklustre.

Back to the drawing board. How could we access the /openidm/managed/user servlet with our path traversal, and still have the path be recognised by OpenIDM? We grabbed the OpenIDM source closest to the version we were testing against and let GPT 5.6 Sol Ultra spend a good six hours on it.

It succeeded. Even though Sam and I had both spent time trying to manually bypass this juvenile restriction ourselves, we hadn't tried every possible combination.

When Sam and I tried /XUI%2f..%2fopenidm/, we got a hard 403, and being human, we assumed that applied to every prefix. What GPT 5.6 found was that /static%2f..%2fopenidm/ wasn't covered by the WAF at all, and it gave us the OpenIDM APIs without the servlet confusion issue.

With this bypass, taking over an account became trivial. We could PATCH a new password for any user profile:

OK, so we could overwrite the password for any user. But what about multi-factor authentication?

Well, the automaker's Australian environment had one more misconfiguration: the multi-factor step could be skipped entirely. First, we requested an authentication token from login.redacted-identity.com.au with a Resource Owner Password Credentials grant, which returned a clean id_token we could swap for something usable in the consumer app:

We took that and moved on to step two, exchanging the first token for a session:

Lastly, we performed the token exchange for a mobile app access token:

It's super convoluted, I know. OAuth is annoyingly hard sometimes. But these steps let us bypass multi-factor authentication for any user of the automaker's connected car app.

At this point, we were so damn close to unlocking any of the automaker's cars in Australia. All that was left was issuing the command API requests with the app token we could now mint for anyone.

We obviously did all of this on cars we explicitly owned or had permission to interact with.

But let's take a step back and think about what we achieved here. We could send commands to any of the automaker's vehicles in Australia, across both of their brands, by only knowing the number plate of the vehicle. The final chain looked like this:

At 12AM, when the full chain finally came together, I took a short video of the script in action. It's not polished, but it still felt magical seeing it all work. I definitely live for hard challenges like these.

You might be wondering: Shubs, you said any of their cars in the world. This only covers Australia. What gives?

There's a much more complex chain that Sam and I worked on together, which he's published on his blog here. It required a lot more work than the steps we took for Australia, as the automaker had spent much more time securing the US, Canadian, and European OpenIDM environments.

At the end of the day, with both writeups combined, we could send remote commands such as unlocking and turning the engines on, for any of the automaker's vehicles in Australia, the United States, and Canada.
