Combative LaGuardia ATC Berates Southwest Pilot Over Taxiway Mistake

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The pilots of a Southwest Airlines flight departing New York LaGuardia (LGA) made a mistake. What’s wild to me is how unprofessionally the air traffic controller handled that mistake, though, and the clear joy he got from being able to chew out the pilots…

YouTube channel You can see ATC has the air traffic control audio and a visualization of an incident that happened shortly after 7PM local time on Tuesday, November 3, 2026, at New York LaGuardia Airport.

A Southwest Boeing 737-800 was getting ready to depart to Dallas Love Field (DAL), and was just getting permission to taxi to the runway, which is when the issues started:

Air traffic controller: “Southwest 4541, runway 13, give way to an E170 off the left, taxi via L, B, G, hold short of runway 22.”Southwest pilot: “Taxi runway 13 via L, B, G, hold short of runway 22, give way to E170 off our left, Southwest 4541.”

The Southwest plane then started taxiing, but immediately cut off the Embraer E170 it was supposed to give way to on the taxiway. That was probably a dumb error (more on that in a bit), but that’s when the air traffic controller really got excited:

Air traffic controller: “Southwest 4541.”Southwest pilot: “Southwest 4541, go ahead.”Air traffic controller: “Did you give way to the 175 we told you about?”Southwest pilot: “Uh, sorry, we were looking at a different one. We’ll still be on B. Are we clear?”Air traffic controller: “Southwest 4541, hold there. Show me the 175 that you were looking out for.”Southwest pilot: “We’re looking at the Delta colors to the left, Southwest 4541.”Air traffic controller: “So that’s the one we told you to give way to and you cut them off, right?”Southwest pilot: “Affirmative, Southwest 4541. Where would you like us to go?”Air traffic controller: “Southwest 4541, possible pilot deviation, advise ready to copy a phone number.”

The Southwest pilot then copied down the number, and then the air traffic controller broadcast on frequency that “Southwest looks like just decided to do whatever they want.”

There are two issues here — the mistake on the part of the Southwest pilots, and how the air traffic controller responded.

Obviously the Southwest pilots screwed up here, as they did cut off the Embraer. As much as Southwest pilots are known for their speedy taxis, I’d be willing to bet this wasn’t intentional, because if you try to fight a NYC-area air traffic controller, you’ll lose every time. 😉

My guess is that the pilots were genuinely confused by the word order of the instructions, and didn’t fully process the instructions until it was too late (which isn’t to excuse it, but just to try to make sense of how this happened).

The instructions were to taxi to “runway 13, give way to an E170 off the left, taxi via L, B, G, hold short of runway 22.” Based purely on those instructions, it wasn’t entirely clear where the Embraer would be, since the instruction to give way was given after the runway assignment.

It seems like a clearer way to describe that would be to give the taxi instructions, and then explicitly say where the plane is that they should be giving way to. So something like “runway 13, taxi via L, B, G, hold short of runway 22, give way to the Delta colors E170 off on your left on A.”

But the real issue was the laser focus that the controller had on just talking down to these pilots and proving a point. He literally stopped traffic so that he could win an argument. By all means give the pilots a number for a possible pilot deviation, and discuss this stuff off frequency. But to stop traffic, argue with the pilots, give them a number, and then throw in another insulting comment, is just uncalled for and incredibly unprofessional.

Compare this to an incident at Boston Logan (BOS) a few weeks back, which I’d argue was more serious. An Etihad Boeing 787 entered a runway without permission and caused a go around. Shocking as it might be to some New York area controllers, the Boston controller didn’t take it as an opportunity to insult someone on frequency, and stop traffic. Instead, he handled it professionally, kept planes moving, and then they got a number for a pilot deviation. That’s how it should be handled.

The pilots of a Southwest Boeing 737 chose the wrong airport to make a mistake at, as it quickly turned into the highlight of the air traffic controller’s day. The air traffic controller stopped traffic so that he could school pilots and give them a number for a possible pilot deviation, rather than keeping planes moving and then discussing this off frequency.

What do you make of this LaGuardia ATC interaction?

NHS breach helpline left staff carrying a 'significant emotional burden'

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Team given a day's notice faced distressed callers, safeguarding decisions during project that stretched from 2 weeks to 11

NHS staff drafted into a helpline after colleagues' health data was exposed online faced distressed callers, safeguarding concerns, and a "serious emotional impact," a review has found.

The report [PDF] comes almost exactly a year after the Royal Cornwall Hospitals NHS Trust disclosed a data breach affecting 8,003 people, including current and former trust staff and non-trust workers.

A member of the public discovered the exposure in November 2025. An Excel workbook published in response to a Freedom of Information request in February 2023 retained underlying sickness absence records that users could reveal through its pivot tables.

Staff assigned to the helpline described difficult calls involving shouting, anger, and distress.

Particularly difficult calls involved people distressed by the sensitivity of the disclosures or potentially in danger from domestic abuse. Call handlers said they had to make safeguarding assessments without training or an established process.

"Such occurrences of anger or distress were not infrequent, and this reflected the heightened emotions and distress experienced by callers to varying degrees due to the breach," the review noted.

"This placed significant emotional burden on individuals working in the contact center, who were not prepared or provided support on how to deal with those."

Members of the trust's clinical governance team were told just a day beforehand that they would staff the helpline. Despite their experience dealing with patient trauma, they received little preparation for the calls that followed.

"The staff working in the call centers had a short training session and then the calls started coming in within minutes of the team being set up," the review noted. "Some staff had not finished reading the pre-prepared script which had arrived only 30 minutes before the center went live."

Most call handlers only learned that their own data had been exposed while already working on the helpline, sometimes minutes before supporting others affected.

Call handlers also said they had little choice about taking on the work and were expected to maintain their usual duties alongside it.

The review stated that some staff members gave up weekends and worked long past their scheduled hours to meet the demands of both roles.

"It's typical NHS, it runs on goodwill," one staffer told the team that compiled the report.

The call center was only supposed to run for two weeks in December 2025, but due to the volume of calls, it was eventually stood down after 11 weeks, in February 2026.

According to officials speaking at a trust board meeting on Thursday, the review and its findings have since been circulated among staff, and published online.

Responding to a Freedom of Information Act (FOIA) request thought to be from a journalist researching a story about stress in the NHS, the Royal Cornwall Hospitals NHS Trust uploaded a spreadsheet to its public FOI Disclosure log in February 2023.

This contained personal data and inadvertently exposed sickness absence information and sensitive health data related to 7,715 staff, 288 non-trust workers, as well as a small number of unidentified records.

Crucially, this spreadsheet was uploaded in a format that allowed users to access the pivot table from which some data was derived. The data in these pivot tables would normally have been too sensitive to be included in FOIA responses.

The first failure was that the request was handled by the trust's e-Rostering team instead of the People Information Unit, which had the expertise in responding to FOIA requests with complex datasets.

The e-Rostering team compiled the response using the team's own data, but incorrectly believed they had stripped the spreadsheet of the sensitive data that would later be exposed.

Standard procedures instructed request handlers to follow several steps to ensure sensitive data was stripped before documents are published, some of which were skipped.

For starters, one FOI officer assigned to the response either failed to run the spreadsheet through Excel's Document Inspector, which formed part of the usual procedures, or failed to act upon its recommendations.

This feature can flag pivot tables and potentially hidden cached data, but cannot determine whether that data is sensitive or automatically remove the cache.

Staff also failed to follow instructions to convert the spreadsheet to PDF. Investigators found that doing so prevented users clicking through to the underlying records, although they could not guarantee that no hidden data remained technically accessible.

At the time, internal guidance did not require conversion to CSV, which would have removed the workbook's pivot-table functionality and embedded cache.

The trust's sign-off process also focused on answering the request's questions and meeting their public relations duties, and falsely assumed that the data had been adequately sanitized before the final review.

The spreadsheet was made available for 33 months, although the trust is not sure how many times it was viewed during that time.

Its logs only covered the 12 months before a member of the public alerted it to the pivot table issue. These indicated that it had been accessed 340 times, although the review noted there was no way of knowing how many of these, if any, led to pivot tables being accessed, or even if it was viewed by a human, with web crawlers adding to the total view count.

The trust has since implemented a number of changes to prevent a recurrence of the breach, including the mandatory password-protecting of any Excel workbook as part of FOIA responses.

A senior information governance officer must now approve spreadsheet responses before executive sign-off. Revised guidance circulated in November 2025 also called for conversion to CSV, although investigators found that this requirement had not been incorporated into the formal operating procedure and recommended updating it.

The review says the Information Commissioner's Office closed its case after the trust implemented its recommendations.

A spokesperson for the Royal Cornwall Hospitals said: "We are deeply sorry for the accidental sharing of sickness absence data which we reported in November 2025, and for the distress and concern it caused those colleagues whose data was involved.

"An independent investigation has now been completed, and the report has been published in full on our website in the interests of transparency and accountability.

"The investigation has helped us understand what happened and identify areas where improvements were needed. We have already implemented a number of measures to strengthen our processes, particularly around the management of Freedom of Information requests and responses.

"The protection of personal information is of the utmost importance to us, and we remain committed to learning from this incident and preventing a similar event from happening again.

"We continue to provide support to colleagues who have been affected." ®

Raccoon with an arrow stuck in her face rescued in Massachusetts

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 7, 2026 10:06 AM EST

Earlier this week, wildlife veterinarian Dr. Priya Patel and her colleagues at the Cape Wildlife Center in Barnstable, Massachusetts had to deal with something they had never seen before: a raccoon with an arrow sticking out of its face.

But the saga really began several days before then, when an individual in Plymouth, Massachusetts called the Cape Wildlife Center to let them know that they had spotted the injured raccoon. The wildlife center team told the caller to contact the local animal control to apprehend the raccoon, who had no intention of making it easy.

It took animal control around one week to catch the trash panda. Patel, who is also the medical director of the wildlife center, tells Popular Science that she was a “very feisty raccoon,” even while inside of her cage. “So we decided to sedate her right away in the cage so that there was less stress for her, and then we were safe getting her out.”

Once the snoozing raccoon had been removed from the cage, the team realized that whatever was sticking out of her face was also sticking out the back of her head. Patel and her colleagues quickly took X-ray images, which revealed the obvious shape of an arrow—possibly a mini crossbow arrow. In her misfortune, the feisty raccoon was actually incredibly lucky. The arrow hadn’t touched her eye or sinus cavity, nor had it fractured her skull.

“We were able to quickly assess that there was no major structural damage to her, and then we were able to remove it,” Patel explains. “And so we cleaned the wound and put some antibiotic ointment on it and started her on antibiotics and paint meds. She just got really lucky, and we were just grateful to Plymouth animal control for not giving up on trying to catch her.”

The raccoon is doing well, and will stay at the wildlife center until the wound heals properly and she completes a course of antibiotics. The team then expects to return her to the wild.

In Massachusetts, it is legal to hunt raccoons. In 2026, the raccoon and opossum hunting season was from January 1 to January 31, and from October 2026 through the end of the year.

House chairs ask Nvidia’s Jensen Huang to explain reported spy link

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

They want a closed-door briefing on 18 November about his contacts with a former Megaspeed executive.

NVIDIA founder and CEO Jensen Huang during his GTC 2026 keynote at the SAP Center in San Jose, 16 March 2026

The request follows a New York Times report on 29 October. The Times said US spy agencies had watched Alice Huang for about a year over her ties to China. Officials feared she could learn about Nvidia’s plans.

The Times also reported that officials did not believe Jensen Huang knew she might be gathering information for China. Neither of them has been charged. The two are not related.

Megaspeed, a Singapore firm that buys Nvidia chips, has said it hired outside lawyers to look into the claims, Forbes reported. US officials have also looked into whether the company helped China get restricted Nvidia chips.

It is not clear whether Huang will attend. Nvidia did not say when asked by Semafor on Friday.

“We’re confident that if the U.S. government believes that any person is a threat to American industry, it would let us know,” Nvidia spokesman John Rizzo told Semafor.

Huang has said no to Congress before. In June, he declined Senator Elizabeth Warren’s invitation to testify on Nvidia’s sales to China. Warren is expected to chair the Senate Banking Committee next year, Semafor reported.

The Warmest Gloves and Mittens for People Who Get Cold Easily

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

(Photo: Courtesy of Columbia, Gordini, and Swany)

If you’re anything like me, winter is a catch-22. On the one hand, it’s the season for skiing, so I love it dearly. On the other hand, I live in a perpetual state of cold hands.

I have pretty poor circulation, and as a result, my fingers and toes turn icy rather quickly. That’s why the gloves and mittens I wear need to be extra warm. I’ve found that while gloves are easier to grip ski poles with, I’ll trade a bit of dexterity for the extra warmth that mittens provide.

If you also suffer from cold hands, then look no further. We partnered with SKI testers and editors to round up seven of the gloves and mittens they have found to be warmest. From heated options to gloves under $100, this is the ultimate list for keeping your fingers toasty even on the coldest of days.

Outdoor Research Sureshot Heated Soft-Shell Gloves

Sizes: XS–XL (unisex) Shell: Leather and nylon Insulation: Recycled featherless 3M Thinsulate, Polartec Power Grid fleece

Pros and Cons + Handy cuff pull loop + Durable goat leather and nylon shell + Fleecy lining – Some break-in required

Testers found Mainers Mitts to be some of the warmest non-heated mittens. The waterproof Gore-Tex liner keeps hands dry and wicks away moisture, while the recycled “featherless” 3M Thinsulate insulation adds plenty of warmth—without the bulk.

While the mittens did require several wears to break in, SKI testers noted that the lack of bulk helped the mittens’ dexterity. Even testers who prefer gloves were impressed by the mitt’s flexibility.

The parent company of Mainers, Tempshield, makes PPE for laboratories involved with cryogenics and has scientists working in temperatures below −200°F, so you can rest assured knowing Mainers knows how to keep your hands warm.

Sizes: 7–11 (unisex) Shell: Leather Insulation: PrimaLoft Gold

Pros and Cons + Soft and durable full leather shell + Padded fingers and back of hand + Velcro cuff easily tucks under jacket – Water-resistant, not waterproof

This LEKI glove is crafted with one of the warmest synthetic materials, PrimaLoft Gold (100 percent polyester). Testers loved how grippy the glove is and gave high marks for nimbleness. Testers also noted the gloves work with smartphone screens.

A neoprene backing and leather shell will protect hands from high wind chill, but it’s important to note the gloves are not 100 percent waterproof. Instead, they’re water resistant, so it’s best to leave them at home on wet spring days.

Sizes: S–XL (men’s); S–L (women’s) Shell: Waxed leather Insulation: Aquablock Down, high-pile fleece

Pros and Cons + Breathable, not clammy + Supple leather + Neoprene undercuff with hook-and-loop closure – Leather is hard to wash

What sets Gordini’s Alp Pro Mitt apart from the competition is its waxed leather, which keeps heat in and moisture out.

SKI testers loved the mitten’s breathability and found that even when temperatures started to rise, hands didn’t go clammy. The waxed leather exterior keeps the elements out and also preserves the mitten’s lifespan so you can use it year after year. The interior is a mixture of down and fleece, so hands remain warm without overheating.

One tester even went so far as to call the mittens one of the nicest they’ve ever worn.

The mitten’s strongest asset is also a weakness: the waxed leather makes them harder to clean, so perhaps use a tissue instead of a mitten for wiping up messes.

Sizes: S–XL (men’s); XS–XL (women’s) Shell: Polyester with goatskin leather palm Insulation: 200g recycled and virgin polyester

Pros and Cons + Great value + Men’s and women’s sizes + Articulated fit – Run small

The PowBound gloves may lack some of the bells and whistles of the more expensive options on our list, but they do have two of the most important factors: weather protection and polyester insulation.

Testers noted that the gloves are likely not going to last as long as some of the others on this list, but they’ll be sturdy for at least a few seasons. Other pros are the wrist straps, so you can guarantee no gloves will be dropped from the chairlift, and a handy clip to keep them from running off solo.

Testers also noted the gloves run a little snug, so when in doubt, size up.

Sizes: 6–11 (unisex) Shell: Cowhide, neoprene cuff Insulation: Foam

Pros and Cons + Removable liners + Ultra burly + Comes with leashes and wrist grommets with carabiner for easy clipping – Requires break-in period

If you look closely, you’ll find Hestra gloves being sported at almost any resort—they’re the popular kid for a reason.

SKI testers voted the Fall Line glove from Hestra “most rugged” because of the materials that go into keeping hands warm and dry all day long. The exterior is composed of cowhide and treated with leather balm to create a barrier against the elements. Hestra gloves also come with a mini tube of said leather balm so you can treat your gloves as needed.

The interior of the glove is composed of brushed polyester and foam, which helps insulate chilly fingers without overheating. Small details, like a seam over the fingertips, help to reduce pressure points, and when you’re holding a pole tightly, that’s a noticeable difference.

The one downfall SKI testers noted was that the glove can feel a bit stiff when fresh out of the packaging, and the Fall Line requires some love before it comes nice and soft.

Sizes: XS–XL (unisex) Battery Life: 8 hours on low, 4 hours on normal, 3 hours on high, 2 hours on boost; dual battery capacity

Pros and Cons + Also available in a mitt + High-quality leather + No battery removal for charging – Charger can get disconnected easily – Not touchscreen compatible

As one of two heated options on this list, we figured we had to mention SKI testers’ best heated glove overall. The Swany Nexus Heated Glove isn’t cheap, but for those who suffer from cold hands, it’s well worth the hefty price tag.

Testers raved about the glove’s ewool, a new technology that delivers heat to each finger, all the way to the fingertips. The PrimaLoft insulation and the leather shell don’t add much bulk. The glove’s lightweight density and soft feel had testers noting that the glove was both the warmest and most comfortable of the bunch.

Best of all, you don’t need to remove the batteries to charge the gloves; there’s a built-in port on the cuff of the glove. Plus, they’re TSA-compliant, so you can keep them in your carry-on bag.

Sizes: S–XL (men’s); S–L (women’s) Battery Life: 8 hours on low, 5 hours on medium, 2.25 hours on high

Pros and Cons + Zippered gauntlet cuff + Very low profile + Exceptionally breathable – Not for the coldest temperatures

While most of the gloves and mittens on this list are made for cold outdoor pursuits, the Sureshot from Outdoor Research is for everyday use. Frigid dog walks, games in outdoor stadiums, and nights by the campfire are all perfect occasions for a heated glove that isn’t meant for extreme temperatures or hardcore activities. Unlike many other heated gloves on the market, these are even touchscreen compatible.

The two-layer woven stretch exterior means the gloves are water- and wind-resistant. And even though the gloves aren’t 100 percent waterproof, the synthetic insulation kept hands warm even when the gloves were wet.

Testers noted that the smaller wrist batteries were barely noticeable, and on the lowest setting, the gloves will produce heat for eight full hours.

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The MovinkPad 11 comes with the Pro Pen 3, a paper-like screen, and a protective case, and the bundle now costs less than the tablet alone.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I’ve tested a fair few drawing tablets over the years, and while the iPad is still the default for many digital artists, Wacom is clearly keen to lure creatives away. The MovinkPad 11 is one of its more interesting attempts, and this deal is a good excuse to take a look.

Wacom is selling the MovinkPad 11 with a case for £402.70 with the code CASE20 at checkout. That’s down from £469.96, a saving of £67.26 (around 14%). The tablet alone is listed at a discounted £429.98, so you’re paying less for the tablet and case together than for the tablet by itself. It’s a handy extra, too, on a tablet that’s made to be tossed in a bag and used anywhere.

The MovinkPad 11 is built around the Pro Pen 3, not a generic Android slate with stylus support bolted on. The 11.45-inch display has a 2200 x 1440 resolution and a 90Hz refresh rate, and the screen is etched glass. If you’ve ever drawn on the slick glass of a regular tablet, you’ll know it can feel like sketching on a bathroom mirror. The textured surface adds a little grip and feels much closer to paper.

The Pro Pen 3 offers 8,192 levels of pressure sensitivity and 60-degree tilt recognition, and it needs no battery or charging. Those numbers look good on a spec sheet, but what I notice more is how naturally it responds to light pressure and follows your strokes.

It runs Android 14 with 8GB RAM and 128GB storage, so you don’t need a computer. You can download drawing apps such as Clip Studio Paint, and Wacom includes a two-year Clip Studio Paint DEBUT licence.

It isn’t the most powerful Android tablet around, and the screen suits sketching and illustration better than big professional workflows, but for that exact art need, it's a better choice than an iPad Air. It’s a portable digital sketchbook, and at £402.70 with the case, it’s easy to recommend.

Wacom’s premium MovinkPad Pro 14 is now 14% off too, and with its protective cover, it's a similar deal, saving £140.05. As my MovinkPad Pro 14 review found, its gorgeous OLED screen and Pro Pen 3 make it a serious iPad alternative.

The surest way to get a scratch from a feline is to go for a forbidden tummy rub. But why do cats hate being touched on their bellies?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Dogs typically love belly rubs and will roll on their backs to get more. With cats, it's a different story: Most are visibly uncomfortable when someone touches their belly, and the same applies to their feet and tail.

When a cat is petted in these areas, its body language often changes. "They might do a quick head turn, looking at your hand, or they might start moving away from you or wiggling around a lot, basically trying to get away from the petting," Mikel Delgado, a cat behaviorist and senior research scientist at Purdue University, told Live Science in an email. "If these early warning signs are ignored, they might escalate to biting, hissing or scratching."

But why do cats dislike it so much when humans touch these specific areas?

Although there has been little formal research into this question, animal behavior experts proposed a few ideas for why cats don't like to be touched in certain areas.

"Cats are prey animals as well as predators and so they can be very protective of areas that are particularly vulnerable to injury during attack, such as the belly area," Sarah Ellis, head of cat mental well-being and behavior at the cat welfare charity International Cat Care, told Live Science in an email.

A cat's belly houses vital organs ‪—‬ such as the liver, stomach and intestines ‪—‬ without the protective bony cage that the ribs provide for the heart and lungs. Although cats have evolved a special feature called the "primordial pouch" —‬ basically a flap of loose skin and fat on their belly —‬ which provides a little extra padding to protect vital organs during fights, most cats still feel threatened when humans touch or rub their belly.

Cats have extra padding on their stomachs to protect their organs.

Dogs have a similar anatomy to cats, but they have to worry less about falling prey to larger animals. Cats, meanwhile, can be hunted by dogs, urban coyotes, and even birds such as hawks and owls, making it natural for them to be protective of their bellies.

Feed your curiosity with an exclusive mystery every week, solved with science and delivered direct to your inbox before it's seen anywhere else.

The tail is also a vulnerable area for cats. "When a cat is chased by another animal, including other cats, the tail is often the part of the cat's body that is closest to touch," Ellis said, "and the risk of being bitten at the base of the tail is high." Therefore, cats may be skittish about having their tail stroked.

When it comes to foot rubs, there are two possible explanations for why most cats hate them. First, a cat's paws are essential in threatening situations. Running away from danger is a cat's preferred survival strategy, and they rely on their paws as a means of escape, Ellis explained. If they can't run away, "a cat may resort to repelling any threats through swiping and scratching and it needs its paws free to do so," she said. "It is therefore predisposed to not enjoy having its paws restricted in any way."

Another reason could be that cats' paws are very sensitive. They have wrist whiskers (also known as "carpal vibrissae") and toe pads that are sensitive to various types of stimuli, like pressure and heat, Delgado said, "so it's not surprising that many cats are sensitive about having their feet touched."

If you're interacting with a cat, it's generally a good idea to stick to the areas where most cats like to be petted: the base of their ears, around their cheeks and under their chin. These areas are rich in scent glands, and cats tend to rub them against other cats they are friends with, Delgado explained. So essentially, cats like being petted in those areas because "they treat us as they do other cats," Delgado said.

But some cats go against the grain. "I've had a few who enjoyed belly rubs," Delgado said. Others enjoy having their tail stroked briefly and show their pleasure "by pushing their rump into the hand of the petter and holding their tail up in the air," Ellis said. (This behavior is sometimes called "elevator butt.")

As puzzling as it may be to humans, these varied preferences are a reminder that "each cat is an individual," Delgado said. Positive responses to petting of the belly, feet or tail can also signal close bonding to a human, Ellis said.

She advises people to "always take the cat's lead, learn what each individual cat likes and dislikes and respect these preferences."

How much of a cat fan are you? Find out by taking our cat quiz!

She learned how to edit scientific papers in a stint as a chemistry copyeditor, before moving on to a medical writer role at a healthcare company. Writing for doctors and experts has its rewards, but Clarissa wanted to communicate with a wider audience, which naturally led her to freelance health and science writing. Her work has also appeared in Medscape, HealthCentral and Medical News Today.

Finland Is Throwing an Epic Dinner Party for 16 Singles—Here's How to Get Invited

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The Nordic countries are well known for their rather gimmicky yet innovative travel campaigns luring travelers to the icy north. From doctors prescribing travel to Sweden as a medical treatment to Oslo's viral mock-documentary narrated by a jaded local called Halfdan. Preceding these by a decade, Danish tour operator Spies Travels famously promoted its ”Do It for Denmark!” campaign focused on Denmark’s low birth rates, offering “ovulation discounts” to couples who proved they conceived on their romantic trip.

Now, Finland has announced it's flying out sixteen international singles this February for a "blind group date with likeminded travelers and local Finnish singles.”

The country is touting the so-called Love At First Bite campaign as an epic blind dinner date, designed to help participants “discover Finnish food, meet new people, and perhaps even find love.” The singles will be divided and hosted in two group dates in capital city Helsinki and Tampere located in Europe’s largest lake district, Finland’s Lakeland region.

Joining the singles over candlelit tasting menus crafted by local celebrity chefs will be local single Finns. Get ready for lots of prolonged eye contact—the Nordic way of flirting—under low light as you bond over the likes of wild trout, reindeer, chanterelle mushrooms, and sea buckthorn. For those heading to Tampere, the destination is monikered the “world’s sauna capital,” so chances of sweating with your fellow single strangers is probably on the menu too.

I’ll admit, when the campaign was launched worldwide, my Finnish friends and I wrinkled our noses in confusion. Romantic and sexy aren’t the first words that come to mind when you think of Finland. And it's not the Nordic country you'd first go to for love if we're talking stereotypes. The Danes have their extroverted charm, the Swedes their chic worldliness, and the Norwegians typically have more money to spend on you. Popular Nordic comedians Helgi and Erlend riff on these regional stereotypes, depicting their Finnish character as stoic, quiet and mentally tough. The very Finnish ethos of “sisu” means grit and the tenacity to push through tough times. But maybe those are qualities you'd want in a lifelong partner as you navigate challenges together.

So the campaign got me thinking a little deeper. Named the happiest country in the world for the ninth consecutive year in a row, Finns are satisfied with their lifestyles firmly rooted in the outdoors, nature, and a slower pace of life. In this contentment is where you’ll find Finland’s hidden sexier side.

In a country of roughly five million, there are two million saunas. And few places are more intimate than sweating together naked in a sauna, dipping in chilly lakes during winter, then warming back up indoors in many more ways. There’s also something rather romantic about grilling wild-caught salmon for two over an open flame in the backcountry.

This isn’t the first time the country has dabbled in an unconventional approach to dating. The viral “Sinkkukori” phenomenon introduced pink shopping baskets as a way for singles to covertly signal their availability. Now Finland is taking a more overt approach to romance. As the well-known nineteenth-century English idiom reminds us, the quickest way to the heart is through the stomach, and Finland certainly wants to put this to the test.

To shoot your shot at finding love in Finland, apply by November 10, 2026 at 11:59 AM Finnish time. All you have to do is make a video answering the question: what would you serve on a dinner date, and why? Just like a proper prospect, Finland would like to know your preferences in advance so it can woo you in the most epic and intentional way. If that isn’t romantic, I don’t know what is.

We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

Here’s Why You Shouldn’t Be Afraid to Harvest Does During the Rut

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 7, 2026 10:00 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

If there’s anything deer hunters can agree on, it’s that we love to argue. There’s plenty to debate: scent control vs. hunting the wind, treestand vs. saddle hunting, compounds vs. crossbows — the list goes on. Unsurprisingly, the advisability of harvesting does during the rut is no different.

Like many hunters this season, I’ve been patiently waiting for any mature deer to slip up during the craziness of the rut. Because, alongside the lone Pennsylvania buck tag in my pocket, are plenty of doe tags. A couple days ago, after passing a few doe fawns and young bucks in hopes that a more mature doe or buck walked through, I began to wonder: How many hunters are purposefully passing does during the rut? And more importantly, is it a good idea to wait or shoot? That’s why I reached out to Lindsay Thomas Jr., chief communications officer of the National Deer Association, for advice.

There are two lines of reasoning on why hunters should or should not shoot does during the rut. The more does running around, one argument goes, the more bucks those does will attract. That camp passes does during the rut because a live doe might walk by and bring a big buck in on her trail, or to the area you’re hunting when she goes into estrus in a few days.

I fall into the other camp. I agree that nothing beats a real doe’s scent — which is why I want to put a doe down nearby. Thomas agrees.

“My personal belief is there’s no better attractant for bucks during the rut than a doe on the ground in your hunting area,” says Thomas. “There is no better attractant. I don’t care what’s in a bottle. I don’t care what calls you’re using, nothing beats it.”

Both Thomas and I have experienced just how successful dropping a doe near your stand during the rut can be. Last year I dropped a doe about 40 yards from my stand early into an afternoon rut hunt. About an hour later, two small bucks came in to check her out, both presenting broadside shots multiple times. I chose to pass those two, but had they been bigger, more mature bucks, one wouldn’t have been so lucky.

“Many other hunters I know have seen this strategy work,” says Thomas. “Dr. Grant Woods has got some great video that he captured showing a buck actually using its antlers trying to get the dead doe to stand back up. By understanding tending behavior, we know that when a buck separates an estrus doe from the other deer, she will typically bed down until she’s ready to breed. Oftentimes this involves the bedded doe laying for upwards of an hour or two just chewing her cud until she’s ready. So, a doe lying on the ground smelling like an estrus doe and looking like an estrus doe is not at all alarming to a buck this time of year. In fact, it’s totally natural.”

If you’re interested in trying to fill an antlerless tag and shoot a buck during the same hunt, it’s important to do your best to put that doe down close to your setup. It’s not unusual for deer to run up to 100 yards after the shot (particularly if you’re bowhunting). In my mind, if a doe piles up that far away, it lowers the likelihood that a buck comes into bow range if he cruises by to check her out.

True, it may not matter because a buck could follow her scent on the exact same trail she took and present the same great shot. It’s also just as likely that another buck cruising through the area catches a whiff of her where she fell and beelines straight to her. If she’s lying dead out of sight, you may never see him. Personally, I get down, tag her, and bring her closer to my setup as quickly and quietly as possible. (Plus, in Pennsylvania, you have to tag the first deer you harvested before filling any other tags.)

Thomas is less inclined to climb out of his stand.

“I don’t think you want to get down and spend an hour blood trailing if it’s not going to be an easy blood trail,” says Thomas. “Walking all around that area is a ‘no’ for me, but if you saw her go down in some brush and know right where she is and you can make it quick, dragging her into the open where she’s visual from more areas might be a good thing for the remainder of your hunt.”

It’s worth noting that it is a hunter’s duty to always ethically dispatch and recover the first animal you shot, so make sure to pick your shots wisely, make smart choices about the recovery, and, if you’re choosing to use the doe as an attractant, only do so on cold days where the meat won’t spoil while you sit for a few more hours.

Regardless of your opinions on rut tactics, the NDA has been a proponent of harvesting does as early in the season as possible to meet your properties management goals. Experts say shooting whitetail does during the rut can also support healthy deer herds. Since one adult doe eats 6 to 10 pounds of forage per day, managing the overall deer population becomes more crucial as winter approaches.

“There are three things that help produce good bucks: age, nutrition, and genetics. We can only control two of those, age and nutrition,” says Thomas. “And by taking a doe as early as possible, or even during the rut, you’re helping enhance nutrition. You’re bringing the overall deer density down [and] producing more forage for other deer to help get them through the winter.”

Read Next: Quit Worrying About Genetics: How Successful Deer Hunters Think About Antler Growth, Cull Bucks, and Passing Good Deer

In short, shooting does early in the season leaves more forage for the rest of the herd, enhancing the health of your overall herd, and enhancing the nutritional output of the habitat for your bucks, resulting in larger, healthier bucks next season. It’s also more difficult to fill doe tags the later you wait into the season as does get spookier and harder to pattern during the late season due to hunting pressure and food availability.

As we enter mid-November, many deer hunters across America are reaching the top of the bell curve of rut activity. In some areas, we may already be on the backside of peak rut. If you’re still holding your buck tag and a few doe tags, get out and hunt. If a big doe piques your interest the next time you’re hunting, you can rest assured that putting your tag on her during the rut is only helping your odds that a big buck might just come into your area to check her out.

Derek Horner is the manager of audience development for Outdoor Life where he manages OL’s social accounts and email campaigns, and shares our stories with the world. He was born and raised in central Pennsylvania where his father and grandfather taught him to hunt and fish. He graduated from Pennsylvania State University in 2018 and still lives in central Pennsylvania.

Holiday Decorations That Take Up Next to No Space to Store

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

100+ Most Unique and Practical Gifts 🎁 for Homeowners and DIYers

Published Nov 7, 2026 10:00 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Tips, tricks & ideas for a better home and yard, delivered to your inbox daily.

The holiday season brings warmth, magic, and, often, a welcome touch of festive cheer to our homes, but for those navigating small floor plans or packed closets, the aftermath can quickly turn into a post-holiday headache. Finding room for bulky holiday decorations can quickly take the joy out of this time of year. Fortunately, you can find ways to set the scene in a way that doesn’t require taking up too much of your precious storage space. From collapsible, fold-flat Christmas trees and re-plantable living trees to accents straight from your backyard or the grocery store, there are plenty of clever ways to celebrate big without cluttering your home.

One way to save space after the holiday is to plant your tree in your own backyard. By choosing a living Christmas tree, you’ll save space, reduce waste, and build memories that last for years to come. A living Christmas tree is sold with its roots still attached, often called a “balled and burlapped” tree. Not only will a living tree fill your home with a fresh pine scent, but replanting it provides environmental benefits, such as minimizing waste, reducing deforestation, providing wildlife habitat, and sequestering carbon. You’ll be able to extend your holiday joy by incorporating replanting the tree as part of your celebration with friends and family, and you’ll be able to enjoy it throughout the year. Look for a living Christmas tree at nurseries, garden centers, online retailers like Lynch Creek Farm and Trees.com, and specialized tree farms.

This classic evergreen makes an ideal Christmas tree—and it can be planted in your yard after the holiday.

Minimize how much space you need to store holiday decorations by purchasing some that inflate or pop up. This Pop Up Christmas Tree, for example, is a smart choice. No assembly is necessary, and it comes fully decorated with Christmas balls, ribbons, bows, and lights already in place. The stand collapses and the tree folds flat, so you can easily tuck it away once the holiday ends. You can also look for inflatables like trees, gift boxes, and snowmen that can be used inside or outside. Once deflated, they’ll be easier to store.

Tapping into nature is one of the easiest ways to decorate for the holidays without accumulating endless bins of off-season storage. By foraging organic materials right from your own yard or neighborhood, you can create a naturally elegant scene. Beyond being completely free and eco-friendly, natural décor easily transitions across seasons or can be safely returned to the earth once the festivities end by adding items to a compost bin, shredding branches into garden mulch, using twigs to kindle a fire in your fireplace or fire pit, creating winter backyard shelters for birds, or setting them out for municipal tree recycling.

Check out these sample natural holiday decorations:

Food can double as holiday décor, from ornaments to a table runner, without requiring any storage space once your guests have left. Either the decor items will get eaten, or you can place them in a compost bin. Here are some ideas for crafting gorgeous, aromatic pieces using simple grocery store items:

Investing in multipurpose décor is the ultimate strategy for keeping your home festive without adding post-holiday clutter to your storage space. By selecting items that easily transition into everyday accent pieces, you can maintain a warm, welcoming vibe long after the holiday season comes to an end.

How Going Blind Made Doris Salcedo’s Art Bigger and Better

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Doris Salcedo is best known for her moving “anti-monuments,” gigantic artworks—or sometimes negative spaces—that propose new ways to commemorate atrocities. Always, she focuses on the victims’ perspectives. Her best-known work, Shibboleth (2007), installed in Tate Modern’s Turbine Hall, comprised one giant, scar-like crack in the concrete, rerouting foot traffic in a manner that evoked the jarring disruption of borders more broadly.

The Colombian artist began working at such a monumental scale only after she began losing her central vision. At age 31, Salcedo was diagnosed with Stargardt disease, a rare genetic condition, and today she is legally blind, with only peripheral sight. Related Articles Sophie Calle, Doris Salcedo Among Winners of $105,000 Praemium Imperiale Award Doris Salcedo Deserves Better Than Her Current Fondation Beyeler Survey

Before her vision started to change, Salcedo made all her sculptures alone. Reflecting on that time during a visit to one of her studios in Bogotá in July, she remembered treasuring “the privacy of improvising and of working directly with the materiality.” But as her condition progressed, she had to give up solitude “little by little” and started working with assistants.

Naturally, she mourned surrendering the spontaneity her independence enabled. At first, Salcedo, now 67, found that “it was a very difficult adjustment, especially losing the privacy.” She also found that she had to “think in a different manner,” since “before, I was improvising; I never had a prior drawing.” When she works with a team, she has to come up with a plan she can clearly communicate: “Things have to be absolutely defined,” she explained. Slowly, though, she found that working collaboratively “gave me the possibility of making better pieces than the ones I was making by myself. A collective statement is far more powerful than a lonely solo voice. It becomes like a chorus, and a really potent one.”

In November, at White Cube Gallery in New York, Salcedo will premiere a sculpture that is the culmination of decades of this collaborative way of working—and huge in a way that could never be accomplished by one person. A full-sized house—at nearly 30 feet—has been woven entirely from human hair. When I visited, dozens of assistants were working across her three studios, tying knots in individual strands, a painstaking process. The resulting pattern resembles chicken wire or a chain-link fence.

Even working with such a big team, the process took three years. “To be thankful is not enough,” Salcedo said of the collective effort, adding that it could have easily taken 20 years to make on her own—and that she couldn’t spare that kind of time at her age.

As Salcedo led me through her studio—her own iconic salt-and-pepper curls rhyming with the looping locks lining the space—she told me that all of her workspaces were designed by the architect Carlos Granada. The two have been working together for 25 years. He is also the architect behind Fragmentos, Salcedo’s 2018 “anti-monument” commissioned by her national government to commemorate the 2016 Colombian peace agreement. Visiting the building in the heart of Bogotá, what at first looks like a typical modernist, single-floor gallery takes on new weight in the feel of its flooring: The textured tiling is made from firearms that once belonged to the Colombian guerrilla group FARC and were later melted down. Visitors walk right on top of these instruments of violence and fear, on tiles Salcedo made in close collaboration with 20 Colombian women who survived sexual violence during the conflict.

The work has an undeniably chilling effect. School groups flock to it regularly. Some critics, though, have found its message misleading, since several splinter factions are still active and thousands of fighters rejected that 2016 agreement. But for Salcedo, Fragmentos is not a static space: She regularly invites other artists whose work resonates to exhibit within its walls. When I visited, a crew was de-installing a sound installation by Hajrah Waheed that filled the space with movingly hummed songs of resistance. The work, titled Hum II (2023), is surprisingly affecting, unifying: You’re made to feel something of the song’s sentiments even if they’re in languages you don’t understand. Fittingly, the title means “we” in Urdu, Waheed’s mother tongue.

Like Fragmentos, Salcedo’s studios boast high ceilings. Her workspaces also feature glass brick walls and floating staircases: These are sparse spaces that enable her and her team to see what they are working on. As she walked me through, magnifying glass in hand, she pointed out that one of her studios is the same shape as the home she is building—a triangle atop a square, as if a kid drew a house. Eventually, we came upon the net-like hair arranged in sections like a gradient, growing darker and denser at the bottom. She let me touch it, and I was surprised to find it so sturdy and lightweight at the same time.

THE WORK-IN-PROGRESS MARRIED a complicated process to a simple shape. As we spoke, I found myself wondering about the latter—why a house? I found a hint in two books sitting on the surface of her desk, both of them scanned so she can see them on a screen in large print: Domicide: The Global Destruction of Home and Dreamhouse: Stories of Art and Shelter.

House is not her first work on the subject of the devastating effects of the global housing crisis. Uprooted (2020–22), her prize-winning work in the 15th Sharjah Biennial (where, as it happens, she met Waheed, a fellow prize winner), repurposed 804 dead trees into a mesmerizing, if uninhabitable, home. At almost 1,000 feet long, the work is meant to speak to the predicament of climate refugees. Her newer work, which takes the same shape, is about more deliberate displacement: specifically, “domicide,” which the authors of one of the books on her desk, J. Douglas Porteous and Sandra E. Smith, describe as the calculated destruction of homes. Explaining the impetus for her piece, Salcedo pointed to current conflicts in Ukraine, Gaza, Syria, and Sudan, where people are being violently displaced en masse.

Domicide, she pointed out, has a cascading effect: victims lose not just a structure but their communities. They lose access to privacy and respite, as well as bathrooms and places to cook and sleep. Mental health, studies show, tends to deteriorate rapidly as victims are forced into survival mode.

With House, Salcedo wanted to show that, while the dispossessed are of course vulnerable, they are also resilient. Her meshy nets are fragile and flexible, yet they are also much stronger than they seem. Hair may be ephemeral, but she makes it structural and sturdy. It’s all inspired, she told me, by the ways that spiders make webs of silk, material they take from their own bodies. Pairing hair and home, she evokes the ways that bodies and buildings—like dignity and domesticity—are tightly bound.

SALCEDO LEARNED TO WORK WITH human hair from earlier pieces: “Unland” (1995–98) comprises tables severed and then rearranged such that they depend on dissimilar tables for structural support. As if personifying the balancing act, the artist intricately threaded the surfaces with hair, drilling tiny holes in the tables to insert locks she gathered from a local salon. But for House, given its magnitude, she decided to source hair online, learning to look carefully for certain certificates, as there is a lot of violent crime in the human hair trade.

Hair is not the only tricky material that will feature in her fall show. On an upper floor of one of her studios, more assistants were at work on objects of a smaller scale, all destined for display alongside House at White Cube. They were wearing jeweler’s glasses and using X-Acto knives to meticulously remove the starch from individual grains of rice. A team of architects and engineers looked on, huddled around a computer on a lofted level as they worked out the structure of the sculpture’s shape. “I’ve never had such a large team,” she explained as I took in the scene. At one point, she tried working with a renowned engineer who eventually stepped down, saying the idea was too complicated.

When an artist uses sculpture’s most stalwart materials like wood or bronze—as Salcedo has in the past—“the tools are already designed for you,” she said. House required more trial and error than she’s ever needed before—so it also required her biggest team yet. “I had to fabricate new tools and new ways of working,” she explained as we watched assistants drill minuscule holes in starchless rice grains before passing them along to the next station, where still other assistants would thread individual hairs through those tiny openings. They were assembling a kind of mesh, then forming it around damaged cooking supplies—bowls and pots and pans—to form Grains of Sorrow (2023–26), a reminder of what is lost to domicide besides simple structure.

If this sounds like an incredibly elaborate—and surprisingly elegant—way to speak to tragedy, for Salcedo, both beauty and labor are part of her point. She is cognizant of how her studio might provide jobs to those who need them, employing, for instance, 150 women from the Colombian countryside, where it is difficult for them to find work.

The decision to both scale-up and work elaborately is as conceptual as it is material: “My work has always been labor-intensive because in the Global South, labor is intensive,” she said. “It’s completely different from industrialized countries.” She meant this not only as a way to shine a light on contemporary conditions, but also as a retort to art-historical tropes: Modernity is too often narrated, she said, as if “no single person from the Global South participated. But we know all the labor and all the suffering created modernity.” She wants to combine the forms and traditions of European sculpture with painstaking labor to show the two were always intertwined.

I asked her about the role beauty plays for her; she replied that she’s received a lot of criticism on this matter. She maintained, however, that “in all the violence and terror I’ve been researching all my life, I think the abject part belongs to the perpetrator,” adding that “beauty has a lot to do with dignity”—something she wants to restore as she tells the stories of victims. Elegance is also a way to get people to really look: The stories she’s telling are almost always in the newspapers, but that doesn’t mean everyone stops to soak them in. Salcedo doesn’t want you to divert your eyes from the world’s horrors, so she goes to extreme lengths to make sure you can’t look away.

Eight galleries artfully made from concrete

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Our latest roundup collects galleries where exposed concrete surfaces form contemplative settings for paintings and artwork.

From high-profile art museums to modest pavilions housing singular artworks, the galleries below illustrate the endless and artful possibilities of concrete.

Among the projects included in the roundup are buildings by Pritzker Prize-winning architects Peter Zumthor, Eduardo Souto de Moura, Tadao Ando and David Chipperfield Architects.

Read on to discover eight concrete art galleries from around the world:

The David Geffen Galleries, USA, by Peter Zumthor

Wrapped in a ribbon of glazing, The David Geffen Galleries is a curving concrete extension to the Los Angeles County Museum of Art, designed by Swiss architect Peter Zumthor.

The elevated gallery snakes above street level and spans Wilshire Boulevard. It is supported by seven structural pavilions that are scattered unevenly along its footprint.

Casa das Histórias Paula Rego, Portugal, by Eduardo Souto de Moura

Two pyramid-like chimneys rise from Casa das Histórias Paula Rego in Cascais, Portugal, which was designed by Portuguese architect Eduardo Souto de Moura to house the works of artist Paula Rego.

Enclosed by red-concrete walls, the interior was designed as a sequence of rooms tailored to different formats, from small lithographs to large-scale paintings.

Xiao Feng Art Museum, China, by ZAO/standardarchitecture

Chinese studio ZAO/standardarchitecture created an angular concrete building looping around a central courtyard for the Xiao Feng Art Museum in Hangzhou.

Dedicated to the artist couple Xiao Feng and Song Ren, the gallery rises and falls to form openings that guide visitors under bridge-like elements to the courtyard.

Japanese architect Tadao Ando designed a subterranean concrete dome to house a permanent installation by British sculptor Antony Gormley at Museum SAN in Wonju, South Korea.

Spanning 25 metres in width, the cave-like space was topped by a central oculus that looms over the cast-iron sculptures.

Nubuke Extended, Ghana, by Baerbel Mueller and Juergen Strohmayer

Nubuke Extended is a one-room concrete gallery extension to the Nubuke Foundation in Ghana, which was raised above the ground to form a shaded events and gathering space below.

Designed by architect Baerbel Mueller and architectural designer Juergen Strohmayer, the gallery’s interior features high ceilings and a mezzanine level.

Richard Serra gallery, USA, by Thomas Phifer and Partners

At the Glenstone Museum in Maryland, American architecture studio Thomas Phifer and Partners created a rectangular gallery for the artworks of sculptor Richard Serra.

The building was made from cast-in-place structural concrete with doors made of stainless steel, and a series of skylights provides the only source of light inside.

Anselm Kiefer Pavilion, Germany, by David Chipperfield Architects

Six years after completing the Carmen Würth Forum museum in Germany, architecture firm David Chipperfield Architects returned to the site to add a monolithic concrete gallery built around a singular large-scale artwork.

Once too big to exhibit at the museum, the Schwarze Flocken artwork by Anselm Kiefer now hangs on the wall of the Anselm Kiefer Pavilion, illuminated by rooflights.

Ginkgo Gallery, China, by MORE Architecture

Scooped concrete ceilings animate the Ginkgo Gallery in Jiaxing, which was designed by Dutch studio MORE Architecture as an antidote to China‘s boom of large-scale museums.

Located on an island in a manmade lake, the museum’s board-marked concrete surfaces were arranged to create horizontal patterns across the walls and roofs.

Talking Point: What Are You Playing This Weekend? (7th November)

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

HYAAH, HUHH, HUP, HYAAH *coughing and splutting* Sorry about that, everyone. Welcome to another edition of What Are You Playing!

Just in case you somehow missed it, it was Zelda week this week as Ocarina of Time finally landed on Switch 2! The ensuing Ask the Developer interviews revealed the game's dev team, Aonuma's reasons for returning to it, Water Temple tweaks and more. There was also a wild midnight launch event in London, Metascore madness, new gameplay footage and more. In short, a lot of Zelda.

That's not all, though. Nintendo's latest financial report gave us updated Switch 2 sales figures, Pokémon Pokopia's next DLC wave got a release date, 'The Elder Scrolls 6' title was finally revealed, and The Pokémon Company continued its mysterious countdown.

Well hey, all that news means it must have been quiet in review land, right? Wrong! We shared our thoughts on Godzilla: Destroy All Monsters Melee Remastered (7/10), Marvel's Guardians of the Galaxy: Encore Edition (8/10), Stellar Blade: Complete Edition (7/10), Zero Parades: Director's Cut (8/10), and a little game called Ocarina of Time (9/10).

Phew. We can take a pretty good guess at what most of us will be playing this weekend, but let's have a look, just in case.

Guide Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Walkthrough (Switch 2 Remake): Guide To The Main Quest, Side Quests & Collectables

Feature "I Will Have Been Here For 30 Hours" - London's Midnight Zelda Launch Was Wild

My GOTY backlog grows by the day, with Pragmata, The Séance of Blake Manor, Well Dweller and more all sitting there waiting to be played. But who am I kidding? I didn't attend that midnight launch for nothing.

There's a part of me that wants to speed through the remake to see as many of the changes as possible before the year is up. There's another part that wants nothing more than to take my time with it and play nothing else ever again. Decisions, decisions, eh?

Look, obviously, I’ve got the cart. However, at the time of writing, it’s still sealed for a couple of reasons: 1) I was fortunate enough to play two hours pre-release, so I already know how good it looks and feels and I’m prolonging the excitement; and 2) we’re in the autumn section of Click Clock Wood in a Banjo playthrough and, being so close to the final quiz/battle with Grunty, I want to cap that off before diving into Hyrule with the kids.

GOTY is also approaching and I’m catching up with Requiem and Mixtape, and there’s a dozen other things on the backburner. How long can I resist the call of Kokiri Forest?

*rips the plastic off with teeth Sunday morning and spends all day playing in pyjamas

Hmmmm… I wonder what I’ll be playing this weekend? Surely it couldn’t be Ocarina of Time? Well, yes it will be. I still have some collectables to grab and medals I want to collect in the Zelda Notes app. Once I’ve made more progress in that, I am so tempted to return to my old OoT saves from the GameCube and 3DS games or even dabble with Majora’s Mask!

After going to the midnight launch in London, I left so inspired after chatting with fellow Hylians and like-minded superfans. Either way, the Zelda bug has taken a big ol’ chomp into me.

I will still be unlocking teams, skills and players in Tape to Tape this weekend. I also plan to start Eve’s epic journey in Stellar Blade Complete Edition. Being a huge fan of the series, it is quite unfortunate I failed to notice the release of Earth Defense Force 5 on Switch 2, but thankfully, NL readers recommendations saved the day, and I am once again quite hooked on blasting giant ants and spiders (I haven’t even unlocked any mechs yet!). Looking forward to E.D.F. 6 next year.

My game of the Week is NOT The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time. Yes, I too will be enjoying this quality remake while fondly remembering how simpler life was 28 years ago, but having one of my favourite GameCube games re-released with a brand new Unreal Engine 5 coat of paint is proving far too enticing. Godzilla: Destroy All Monsters Melee Remastered is my game of the week, and since there was also a surprise Switch 1 release, everyone should be able to (re)discover the joys of smashing a building into King Ghidorah (and do remember to ask the faeries for Mothra’s help, even if that sounds primitive and unscientific).

I’ve played a hell of a lot of Ocarina of Time recently, so I’m actually keen to play literally anything else. I’ve got my eye on Queen’s Domain: I’ve heard great things about it, and the visual style really speaks to me.

I also recently picked up Alone in the Dark via the PS5’s Black Friday sale, so I’d like to give that a go at some point. Some of the more middling reviews put me off in 2024, but with the cheeky discount, it’s about time I dive in. After jogging around Hyrule for so long, I’m ready for some comforting survival horror.

Those are our plans, but what about yours? Let us know what you'll be slapping in your Switch (2) in the poll below.

Jim came to Nintendo Life in 2022 and, despite his insistence that The Minish Cap is the best Zelda game and his unwavering love for the Star Wars prequels (yes, really), he has continued to write news and features on the site ever since.

I am COMPLETELY absorbed by World of Warcraft Forever. I doubt that I will play anything in the coming months ... sorry Zelda.

I finished The Lives Of The Necromancers by William Godwin (good, but hard going!), and am now reading The First Ghosts by Irving Finkel, a study of how ghosts were viewed in Mesopotamian culture.

ZOOT - Yeah, that's how I refer to it. 😆 It was hard to ignore this one. Not sure how I feel about it so far, so I won't comment just yet.

Banjo Kazooie (Evercade) - I have really been enjoying this! It's certainly a game which stands the test of time, and the improvements are good. Shame that you can't save within worlds without being kicked back to the hub, an improvement that I was really hoping for (they did that for ZOOT!), but it's still a beguiling game. Oh, and, for those interested... the Evercade Nexus is fantastic so far. A solid upgrade.

Have a lovely weekend, guys, and please take great care of yourselves out there.

Honestly, I’m having an absolute blast. There’s something so magical about being back in Hyrule, and I’m trying to savour every minute of it.

Something cool that I noticed is that the controller vibrates when playing the ocarina. The low D is a D, the F is an F, but the A sounds like another D on the controller, and the B sounds like an E and the high D sounds like a G. I guess it can't go very high, as it sounds like it struggles when I try to play anything higher than the high D (controller G). Strangely, it sounds like a B when I hold down on the stick and Z on the low D, but trying it on the A (controller D) sounds like it's partway between C and C#. Because only the top three notes are different, songs like Zelda's Lullaby and Epona's Song sound similar—just in a different key—from the controller, but songs that also use the lower two notes sound very strange, like Saria's Song being F D E. I can't use a tuner as it's too quiet so I might be completely wrong about this, haha... I'm kidding; my ears aren't that bad, I think...

I'm still playing Fire Emblem: Fortune's Weave, so I won't be playing Ocarina of Time anytime soon.

I gotta say, I am loving the Increased Class Exp boon. Quicker class mastery allows for early access to class exclusive abilities, which you can then experiment its synergy with other classes and abilities.

Lysander for example is my favorite creation with the most rewarding experience. First, I look at his personal skill, Racing Attack, which grants Avoid +15 when initiating combat. The Speed stat is what determines the Avoid rate, so I checked to see what Advanced Class grants him high Speed growth. Having completed Dietrich's path in Part I, I had access to the Ranger class boosting Lysander's Speed growth rate to 65. With the Ranger class in mind, I invested Lysander in archery while learning abilities that enhanced Avoid rate. I gave him the refined Tempest Bow+1 for passive Avo +10 and +3 when initiating battle, the In Shadow ability for one turn Avo +10 after defeating a foe, Dodge for Avo +5 against magic/bow users, and Regroup for Avo +20 when a foe avoids an attack. I thought Regroup would be useful since Lysander's accuracy averages around 80-90%, but in practice it was hardly ever used in combat nor necessary when foes couldn't counterattack in the first place. I could've given him the Phantom Ring accessory for Avo +5, but I wasn't sure if adding +3 Weight would make it worthwhile. However, the most unexpected bonus is Lysander's secondary exclusive ability, Accelerate, which grants extra speed for every kill. More speed means better avoid rate (and Attack Speed (AS)). As of now, Lysander has an initial Avoid value of 60. Combined with his abilities, he's nearly untouchable when foes struggle to hit him with their average hit rate of 10-30%.

My second favorite experiment is Theodora. She has naturally good growth rates on HP, Strength, and Defense and her personal skill Royal Resolve halves all damages when activated. It would seem natural for her to pursue armored classes, until I realized something about her Blaze Art. Their range. Her second Blaze Art has an attack range of seven. This reminded me of the Ballistician class back in Fire Emblem: Shadow Dragon, with their absurdly long-range attacks but abysmal movement. When I promoted Theodora to an Armored Knight, her low movement made her struggle to get involved in the thick of battle and low Resistance made magic users a threat to her. At first, I reclassed Theodora to the Wing Soldier class to boost her Resistance, but I also thought it would be funny if she could become a flying artillery. Wait. That actually sounds like a good idea. I also realized in battle that Theodora fills the niche of a bulky flying unit. Having completed Cai's path in Part I, I have access to the Dragoon class. I also gave her the Brace ability after mastering the Guardian class beforehand. Finally, as a Dragoon she can move across the map wherever she please. Combining high movement with long range attacks is a thing of beauty. She's basically a flying tank capable of neutralizing hard to reach targets. She could also charge right into enemy ranks after achieving Overblaze status, aggro a crowd of enemies towards her, and then dish out lethal counterattacks which would only boost her defense even more thanks to her Brace ability.

AI teddy for three-year-olds gave unprompted self-harm reply, BBC finds

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The BBC could not find who makes the £75 plush toy, which is sold online in the UK.

An AI plush toy marketed for children as young as three gave a self-harm crisis reply to a question that had nothing to do with self-harm, a BBC investigation found. The BBC published its findings on Thursday.

The toy, called Doubao, is sold online in the UK as My Baby Bunny for £75. A BBC Wales reporter asked it a series of questions about how babies are made. When she asked it to define the word sexual, it appeared to mishear her.

“I’m really sorry you’re feeling this way. I can’t help with killing you,” the toy replied, according to the BBC.

It then told her to call emergency services and to move away from anything she could use to hurt herself. When she said she had not mentioned self-harm, it apologised.

The BBC could not find out who makes Doubao. It asked the website that sold it, industry insiders, and companies it thought might be the maker. As a result, it could not ask anyone to explain the reply.

The BBC also tested Grem, a $99 toy from US firm Curio. Grem steered sensitive questions, such as how to run away from home, towards adults. Curio told the BBC that child safety guides its product development.

Paul Clarke, founder of Southampton-based AI toy maker Play Tech AI, said the result did not surprise him.

“We’re crying out for the industry to be regulated,” Clarke told the BBC.

Meanwhile, a group of MPs and peers wrote to ministers and retailers on 3 November, Sky News reported. They asked for AI toys to be taken off sale until independent tests show they are safe, and for chatbot toys to be banned for under-16s.

The UK government told the BBC that children’s safety is its top priority. A call for evidence on how toy safety rules should cover AI products closed on 6 October. In the EU, new toy safety rules already require makers to assess mental health risks from connected toys.

Alina Maria Stan builds connections that people actually feel. As co-founder and COO of Tekpon, she turns product intuition into real moment (show all) Alina Maria Stan builds connections that people actually feel. As co-founder and COO of Tekpon, she turns product intuition into real moments of discovery, shaping how teams find and adopt SaaS every day. Since 2020, she has led Tekpon’s brand voice, media strategy, and growth plays with a clear focus on human outcomes behind every metric. Before Tekpon, Alina followed curiosity across industries and countries. She was CEO of King Casino Bonus and led affiliate and brand strategy at Extremoo Media and Fable Media in Denmark, where she learned how to build partnerships that last. Early on, she sharpened her CRM and pricing instincts at K.H. ApS, always asking why customers choose what they choose. Her approach is rooted in more than a decade of international experience and two master’s degrees, one in Sustainable Consumption from the Technical University of Munich and one in Consumer Affairs Management from Aarhus University.

Spain face England decider without Rodri, Olmo and Eric García as three uncapped players called up

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Spain go into the Nations League decider for top spot against England without the injured Barcelona trio of Rodri, Dani Olmo and Eric García. Luis de la Fuente has handed first senior call-ups to two Betis players and to Real Madrid academy product Jacobo Ramón.

Víctor Muñoz is the fourth player ruled out through injury. Spain arrive in form. They came from behind to win 2-1 in Croatia on 6 October, with Mikel Merino scoring both goals. That made it 11 wins in a row. England beat Czechia 3-0 on the same night, with Harry Kane scoring twice.

Valles, 29, will compete for a place in goal. He has played every minute for Betis this season: 11 league games and all four of their games in the top European competition. Roca turns 30 on 26 November, and his call-up follows a long run of injuries. Jacobo Ramón, the third newcomer, plays in defence for Como and came through the Real Madrid academy.

Valles is one of four goalkeepers in the group. Barcelona’s Joan García is also back between the posts after injury kept him out of the previous squad. Pedro Porro, Nico Williams and Ferran Torres return for the same reason.

Pablo Barrios of Atlético and Ander Barrenetxea of Real Sociedad are also recalled.

Some familiar names are missing for other reasons. Marcos Llorente is absent because he has retired from international football.

The absences in midfield are the most striking. Without Rodri and Olmo, the call for Roca gives De la Fuente another option in the centre of the pitch. Pedri and Fermín are both included.

Several of the squad will be in action on Sunday before joining up. Atlético host Barcelona at the Riyadh Air Metropolitano on Sunday 8 November at 21:00. It is LaLiga matchday 12.

The game carries real weight at the top of the table. Barcelona lead the league on 30 points. Atlético have won their last seven league matches and can go top with an eighth.

Timber Stoves Scout Review: Wood-Fueled Warmth From a Tabletop Heater

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 7, 2026 9:30 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

My family spends a lot of time on our deck, and even when fall evenings start getting chilly, we still find ourselves wanting to be out there. To extend our outdoor living window, we’ve traditionally kept a basket of blankets available by the door and a stack of wood ready by the fire pit. But since building a fire isn’t always convenient, and huddling under a throw isn’t always sufficient, it’s not long before we’re back inside—enjoying our gas fireplace and worrying about the added energy cost only after the bill arrives.

Once we got it set up, Timber Stoves’ Scout tabletop pellet heater provided the perfect solution to our cold-weather woes. After spending many an evening over the last month enjoying eye-level fires that start in just a few seconds, here’s what we love about the unit and what you deserve a warning on before you buy your own.

Bottom line: The Timber Stoves Scout is a stylish, effective solution for outdoor warmth, offering ease of use and maintenance despite initial setup challenges.

Before we started testing the Scout, we definitely had our reservations. In a home with two high-energy dogs and a revolving door of teenagers, was it even safe to have a wood pellet stove on our picnic table? Then there was assembly, which was challenging at first. The instructions weren’t super clear, so we struggled to get the chimney to fit over the main chamber. We ended up using a flat-head screwdriver to nudge open the gap that runs the length of the chimney, and that provided the fraction of an inch it needed to slide on. We also weren’t sure whether to insert the self-tapping screws to keep everything connected (the instructions said yes, but an online video we found implied no—we went with yes). Even getting the pellets to light took a few tries, but once we added a couple of small pieces off some GreenSpark fire starters we had, they took right off.

The good news is that once we got that first fire lit, our worries were a thing of the past. The Scout has an absolutely charming aesthetic, and we quickly fell in love with its functionality as well. The viewing window is perfect for watching your own tiny fire while you share a meal or play a game, and once it’s cranking, it puts out impressive warmth over about a 5-foot radius. We actually got too hot the first night we used it (early evening temps were still in the low 60s), but all we had to do was stop adding pellets from the hopper, and it slowly died down on its own.

Beyond its impressive heat, the Scout tabletop pellet heater features a rugged design that’s clearly built to last. While we weren’t sure it would be safe to have a tall wood pellet stove up off the ground, the weighted base has kept it rock-solid and stable no matter how many times our dogs have crashed into the table. I definitely wouldn’t leave small children unsupervised around an appliance like this, but that’s standard for any fireplace or fire pit.

Upkeep has been a pleasant surprise as well. The dedicated ash collection tray makes cleanup quick and easy—you just dump the cooled ash between uses to keep air flowing freely through the chamber and add fresh pellets to the hopper. After enjoying at least a dozen fires over the past few weeks, the stainless steel body has taken on a pretty purple-and-brown patina in many places, and I’ve simply wiped off any soot that’s collected inside the viewing window with a dry paper towel. All this to say, if I had to assemble it all over again to get to where I am today, I’d do it in a heartbeat.

All You Need to Care for Your Lawn & Garden

Keeping your grass green and your plants thriving doesn’t just take a green thumb—it starts with the right tools and supplies.
