The new MacBook Pro's touchscreen isn't even it's best feature

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Explore projects, trends and inspiring design work.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

They said they wouldn't do it. A touchscreen MacBook has been rumoured since the dawn of time (well, the noughties), but, in a quote that's been infinitely dredged up across the internet over the last couple of weeks, Steve Jobs himself decried the concept as "ergonomically terrible". But Jobs also famously asked, "Who wants a stylus?" And the answer among millions of Apple Pencil users seems to be a resounding, "Us!"

And after a few days with the MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display, I'm happy to report that this is very much a device—perhaps even the ultimate device—for creatives. But rather than the ability to touch the screen, it's all about the quality of that 'PROLED' display itself. Turns out there's a reason Apple decided to lead with that, and not touch, in the name of the device.

Simply put, it's the best laptop screen I've ever seen. With up to 2000 nits of peak brightness, 10,000,000:1 contrast, and P3 + Adobe RGB colour, this is a best-in-class display that puts every other OLED laptop screen I've used to shame. It gives creatives a portable reference-quality display, which means it recreates video, colour and contrast as faithfully as possible. Once you've used the MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display, everything else looks washed-out in comparison, and for that reason alone it's impossible not to recommend for creatives – so long as the small matter of the $2,899 starting price isn't a barrier.

When it comes to touch, as I've written before, in my testing I've found it to be a nice-to-have rather than a need-to-have. Now that we're all touchscreen natives (which wasn't so much the case when Jobs made those comments in 2010), certain navigational actions just feel right, even on a laptop. Scrolling through windows, pinching to zoom in on a photo or map, scrubbing through the timeline in Final Cut Pro; there are plenty of times when touching the screen feels more intuitive than reaching for the trackpad. And Apple's subtle UI tweaks, such as having navigational icons expand when touched, make the whole thing feel more natural.

But equally, there were plenty of times when I preferred to use the trackpad – particularly when a certain level of accuracy was required. And despite Apple claiming to have produced a newly designed hinge with greater stability, it still wobbles. There's just no getting around the fact that a touchscreen on a vertical display is less ergonomic than it is on a flat one.

From a design perspective, the new MacBook Pro isn't a game-changer – unless you really missed the illuminating Apple logo (another noughties mainstay). There are improvements here and there; the Dynamic Island takes up less space than the notch, and slightly smaller bezels result in slightly larger viewing areas. But perhaps the most meaningful design change is the reduction in weight. That OLED panel has allowed for a 14% weight loss – making the 14-inch model, somewhat ironically, lighter than the 15-inch M5 MacBook Air.

But everything pales next to the screen. The PROLED XDR looks incredible. If you're looking for the most impressive visual fidelity available on a laptop, here it is. You can touch it if you like.

The MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR display is released tomorrow, with preorders available now. Prices start at $2899 for the 14-inch model, and $3399 for the 16-inch model.

Suspicious prediction market activity seen before election poll release: NYT

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Bets were placed shortly before two recent New York Times/Siena political polls were released to the public, according to a report.

Suspicious prediction market bets were placed shortly before two recent New York Times/Siena political polls were released to the public, according to a report.

Vanderbilt University researchers said a Polymarket user bet almost $200,000 on Oct. 31 that Nebraska Senator Pete Ricketts would be re-elected. An hour later, a Times/Siena poll was released showing Ricketts in the lead.

A Friday New York Times report said while it was not clear if the user had access to inside information, they won more than $77,000.

The user also placed similar large bets on other markets, including Kalshi, the report said.

“There’s a series of circumstances here that it’s hard to write it off as a random occurrence,” said Brett Goldstein of the Vanderbilt Institute of National Security. It “requires further investigation.”

Polymarket said it had spotted the trades and was investigating. Kalshi declined comment.

Cointelegraph reported in April that a US Army soldier had been charged after making more than $400,000 by betting on a prediction market allegedly using nonpublic information tied to a US operation involving then Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro.

In a statement, the Department of Justice alleged that Master Sergeant Gannon Ken Van Dyke was involved in the planning and execution of Operation Absolute Resolve, which led to the capture of Maduro at a residence in Caracas, Venezuela, in January.

Van Dyke allegedly used this information to buy “yes” shares in related Polymarket contracts, including “Maduro out by January 31” and “Trump invokes War Powers against Venezuela by January 31,” according to the DOJ.

The volleyball World Cup logo looks... wrong

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Crafting a logo for a sports tournament is no easy job. It has to appeal to sports fans, who are notoriously conservative. It's going to have to work at tiny sizes, on a phone screen, and at massive sizes like a billboard or a stadium banner. The best sports logos manage all of the above.

It also has to make its point quickly and efficiently, because ordinary (non-designer) people are barely going to give it a second glance. So it usually pays to make the central visual idea blindingly obvious.

With this in mind, when I first saw the logo for the 2027 FIVB Men’s Volleyball World Cup, my reaction was: "Er… what that's supposed to be exactly?

Representing the tournament taking place in Poland next September, the logo emblem is a tall white shape with spikes at the top, a cluster of small squares in the middle and wavy stripes flaring out towards the base.

Perhaps I'm missing something, not being a volleyball fan myself. But to my eyes, it didn't look like... anything.

According to the press release, the inspiration was the World Cup trophy, whose “industrial form” has been distilled into a minimalist mark. And sure enough, when you look at the very distinctive trophy, it does look a lot like it. But it's not a pure representation, and that's deliberate.

Instead, four ideas are said to come together in the logo: the silhouette of the trophy, a circle representing the Earth, a sweeping curve representing the net, and reaching hands and a rising ball for the players. In the press release Maciej Bogusz, marketing director for the local organising committee, also added a fifth: the fans, represented by a wave rippling through the base.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Once you stare at the logo for a bit and pick out all these elements, yep, they're all present and correct. But that's kind of the problem: you have to stare to pick them out. All these concepts don't just magically mesh into one simple design; indeed, there are so many, that feels like it'd be close to impossible.

At the same time, it annoys the heck out of me when people judge a logo on first sight, because that's not how a logo should be judged. A logo is not primarily designed to be liked on first sight, it's designed to work over time.

The history of design is littered with logos that attracted a wave of negativity on first release, yet ended up being loved over time. And I don't want to make the same mistake here.

A post shared by Polska Siatkówka (@polskasiatkowka_official)

So here's something positive I can say: this doesn’t look like any other sports logo I can think of. There’s no generic shield, no generic ball with a swoosh, no cliches of any kind really.

Remember the London 2012 logo? It was widely ridiculed at first, but by the time the Olympic Games started, it had become a welcome part of the furniture.

This design now has plenty of time to do the same. Its first big outing is on 3 December, when the draw will take place 125m underground in the Wieliczka Salt Mine (because why not, eh?). And when the games start in Kraków, seven months later, the shape that looks so wrong to me may look, well, entirely right.

Honestly, I’m not convinced yet. But ask me again in September. My answer may be very different.

Tom May is an award-winning journalist specialising in art, design, photography and technology. He is the author of the books The 50 Greatest Designers (Arcturus) and Great TED Talks: Creativity (Pavilion). Tom was previously editor of Professional Photography magazine, associate editor at Creative Bloq, and deputy editor at net magazine.

The Sky Today on Saturday, November 7: Venus meets the Moon

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Looking for a sky event this week? Check out our full Sky This Week column.

Venus is already primed to capture attention above the eastern horizon before sunrise. This morning, the Moon passes 1.1° due south of Earth’s sister planet at 7 A.M. EST.

You can easily catch the pair an hour before sunrise, some 6° high in the east and climbing as the sky begins to brighten. Magnitude –4.5 Venus blazes to the upper left of the delicate crescent Moon, whose western limb is just barely lit as the Sun sets across the lunar nearside. Look for earthshine on the already-shadowed portion of Luna’s face, as sunlight bouncing off Earth lights it up.

Near Venus is another bright light: This is Spica, Virgo’s alpha star, shining at magnitude 1.0 and also lingering in the morning twilight. This morning it lies 1.3° northeast of Venus, with the planet and star roughly side by side above the horizon. Use this view as an anchor point: By the 10th, Venus will have moved to stand due west of this star and will be located to its upper right.

Sunrise: 6:36 A.M.Sunset: 4:51 P.M.Moonrise: 5:01 A.M.Moonset: 3:40 P.M.Moon Phase: Waning crescent (1%)*Times for sunrise, sunset, moonrise, and moonset are given in local time from 40° N 90° W. The Moon’s illumination is given at 10 P.M. local time from the same location.

Alison Klesman is senior editor of Astronomy magazine. She holds a Ph.D. in astronomy and has studied a variety of topics, from minor planets to supermassive black holes.

19-year-old Southampton starlet showcasing exciting potential in European loan after early transfer interest

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Southampton sent eight players out on loan this campaign. With a mixture of academy talents and players who are on their way out of St Mary’s, the Saints are seeing mixed success from the departed members of the squad.

Moses Sesay, Nicholas Oyekunle and Jay Robinson were the three teenagers to leave for the season. It’s the latter who Tonda Eckert will be monitoring closely after a breakout term last year in the Championship.

Robinson made 28 appearances for Southampton, scoring twice and providing an assist. Minutes were always going to be hard to come by for the 19-year-old this season. With a move to Monza secured, the attacker has plenty to prove in the Serie A.

Securing a loan move to Serie A is a positive sign of how much potential Robinson has to unlock. It’s safe to say that despite a slow start to life at Monza, the 19-year-old is becoming a key player for the Italian outfit.

The Southampton academy graduate had to wait until September to make his first start, and he impressed in a 1-0 win against Torino. Featuring in an attacking midfield role, he completed the most dribbles in the game with four and had nine touches inside the opposition’s box.

His first goal came in the next match, in which he completed 78% of his passes and was dispossessed only once. Former Saints boss Ivan Juric has shown full faith in Robinson, starting the teenager in three of the last four outings.

The 19-year-old’s best display came against Cagliari, where he scored once in a 4-0 win. Two out of his three dribbles were successful in the game, and he made three line-breaking passes. It has been an impressive loan stint for the youngster with Inter Milan registering their interest.

Southampton have the option to recall Robinson from his loan spell in January. However, given his promising spell so far, the Saints would be reckless to do so.

At left-wing, the South Coast outfit currently have the options of Leo Scienza and Sam Edozie, as well as Lewis Dobbin, who can play there too. Edozie is likely to depart in January, though, after putting in a transfer request late in the summer window.

This would ultimately leave Eckert’s side short of attackers in that position. However, they must avoid recalling Robinson to improve their depth. Even if Edozie were to leave, his exit would give Dobbin a better chance of breaking into the first team after just 409 minutes played.

Most importantly, it would do no favours to the 19-year-old’s development. The Southampton prospect has only recently become settled at Monza, and with his minutes increasing over the last couple of months, the South Coast outfit can’t afford to ruin his crucial playing time.

Chelsea will be without Lauren James for the rest of the current run of fixtures after a scan confirmed a hamstring injury. Sonia Bompastor gave the news at her press conference on Friday, ahead of Saturday’s WSL meeting with Everton at Stamford Bridge (12:45 UK time).

The England forward pulled up during last Sunday’s 6-0 win over Charlton Athletic at The Valley. She lasted 35 minutes before Erin Cuthbert came on in her place, and at the time Bompastor could say little beyond the fact that the problem was in the hamstring. The follow-up scan has now given Chelsea a clearer picture, and it is not the one they wanted.

“LJ will be missing the games until the end of the block,” Bompastor said, as reported by DAVE Sport. That rules James out of the Everton game and of next weekend’s home clash with Manchester City, the side sitting just behind Chelsea at the top of the table on goal difference. According to BBC Sport, she is set to miss at least five matches in total.

Bompastor’s team have won seven league matches in a row and have scored 18 goals across their last four WSL outings, with every player who featured at Charlton trying at least one shot. The squad’s depth in attack will now be tested through a demanding stretch that includes a title rival at Stamford Bridge.

Our Sun’s Undiscovered Stellar Nemesis Is Still Out There, Says Physicist

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Long period, high inclination comets of the sort that can cause extinction level impacts here on Earth have long been thought to originate from a theoretical Oort Cloud. That is, a roughly spherical cloud of icy cometary objects that number into the trillions at distances that extend up to 1.6 light years away.

More recently, Richard Muller, a well-known professor emeritus of physics at the University of California Berkeley, put forth the idea that long period comets that visit our inner solar system are not just caused by passing stars perturbing the outer edges of the Oort Cloud. But they are also perturbed by a low mass red dwarf star (dubbed Nemesis) that along with our Sun is orbiting around a common center of gravity.

Due to Nemesisâ slightly eccentric oval orbit around the Oort Cloud, it periodically perturbs billions of comets that surround our outer solar system.

Every time Nemesis gets closest to the Oort Cloud is when the comets kick in and this comet shower lasts for maybe a million years, Muller told me via phone. That's when Nemesis is in the densest part of the comet cloud, and that's when the number of deflected Oort Cloud comets into the inner solar system are at a maximum, he says.

Just to be clear, this is not any sort of Planet X. This also doesnât have anything to do with random stellar interlopers that sometimes pass close enough to perturb our Oort Cloud and stir up a plethora of inward bound comets.

In contrast, there is nothing random about Nemesis. This is a phenomenon with a clear periodicity. According to Muller, itâs responsible for 250 million years of clearly defined periodic Earth impacts.

In a 1982 paper in the journal Science, two paleontologists, David Raup and Jack Sepkoski, collected the most extensive record of marine extinctions ever and if you plot those extinctions, you see there's a strong 26-million-year period in the data, says Muller.

The mysterious and hypothetical Oort Cloud is thought to be the home of long-period comets that visit the inner Solar System. Image Credit: ESA. LICENCE: ESA Standard Licence

One of the peaks was at 66 million years ago, the KT boundary extinction which wiped out the dinosaurs.

The challenge was to come up with an explanation, says Muller. I said, "Well, maybe there's a companion star that comes close in to the Oort Cloud every 26 million years, and then triggers a comet hitting Earth," he says.

It's an idea that Muller still very much supports, although he isn't sanguine about being able to directly observe Nemesis.

Despite what others seem to think, there is no clear evidence against this hypothesis, says Muller.

The Oort Cloud, a heretofore unseen, largely spherical cloud of comets and small icy bodies left over from the formation of our solar system some 4.5 billion years ago, very much remains a mystery.

There are three sources for the original material that formed the Oort Cloud, Astrophysicist Sean Raymond, Director of Franceâs Bordeaux Astrophysical Laboratory, told me via email. Planetesimals that originated in our Sun's planet-forming disk; planetesimals that originated around other stars in our Sun's birth cluster, and interstellar objects that were captured during stellar encounters or by scattering by Jupiter, he says.

Why hasnât Nemesis already been directly detected?

Many astronomers assume that if Nemesis is an ordinary red dwarf, somebody would have seen it, and the answer is no, because itâs moving with the Sun around the center of gravity. And as a result, Nemesis doesnât show enough proper motion, or movement across our line of sight, to be detectable.

But it must be big enough to perturb the Oort cloud, which means itâs probably at least five percent the mass of the Sun, says Muller.

The consensus view is the K-T extinction event, which impacted the Yucatan Peninsula some 66 million years ago, was most likely caused by a large asteroid.

Yet the evidence that it's an asteroid rather than a comet is very weak, so people say asteroid because that's what Walter Alvarez, a professor of Earth and Planetary Science also at the University of California, Berkeley, said in his initial K-T Boundary paper, Muller notes. But when I asked Alvarez, I said, "You said asteroid. Could it be a comet?" He said, "Sure, we couldn't tell the difference."

The best near-term chance of characterizing this massive cloud, however, comes from the Rubin Observatory, which is just coming online. Astronomers hope that Rubin will be able to detect more long period Oort Cloud comets.

The next comet shower that could cause serious damage to Earth should not be for another 12 to 15 million years from now, says Muller.

The bad news is that if it's out there, Nemesis almost certainly would lie close to the plane of the ecliptic, which means that from our point of view, it would lie co-planar to most of the bodies of the outer solar system. And as a dim 8th or possibly 9th magnitude star, this would make it even more difficult to spot, certainly impossible to see with the naked eye.

So, Nemesis really is probably lost in a field of view that includes a huge number of other stars, says Muller.

Over the last three and a half decades, award-winning science journalist Bruce Dorminey --- a former Forbes senior science contributor, former Hong Kong Bureau Chief at Aviation Week and Space Technology magazine and former Paris-based technology correspondent for the Financial Times, has written for a multitude of high profile publications. They include Astronomy Magazine, Sky and Telescope, Scientific American, Science, Nature News, National Geographic, and Universe Today. He is the author of the 2001 book "Distant Wanderers: The Search for Planets beyond the Solar System." He was also the host of the "Cosmic Controversy" podcast which is still available via Podbean.

atelier gabu hand-builds observation tower on french hillside using timber and translucent fabric

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

facing a medieval stone ruin in vaudémont, france, atelier gabu completes brunebasse open-grid timber observation tower for l’écho du marteau.

Atelier GABU edited by christina vergopoulou November 7, 2026 3 min

Designed and built by architecture studio GABU for the 2021 edition of L’Écho du Marteau, a festival dedicated to architectural experimentation, Brunebasse is a temporary observation tower sited on a hillside in Vaudémont, northeastern France. Positioned directly opposite the heavy stone remains of Brunehaut, a medieval ruined keep, the installation establishes a formal counterpoint to its historic neighbor. Rather than replicating the monument’s defensive masonry and mass, the project employs a light, permeable structural timber grid that prioritizes panoramic visibility over interior enclosure.

Atelier GABU’s architects hand-assembled the elevated timber viewing platform without mechanical tools, framing the structure as a case study in material economy and low-impact fabrication. Slender dimensional lumber is joined through direct mechanical connections to establish an open tectonic frame. Sections of the exterior envelope are wrapped in a semi-transparent textile veil that filters natural light while maintaining visual transparency toward the adjacent fields and tree canopy. Throughout the day, the shifting sun projects the diagonal bracing members as geometric shadow patterns onto the fabric scrim, rendering the structural logic legible from both the interior deck and the exterior landscape.

Brunebasse is a temporary timber observation tower designed and hand-built by Atelier GABUsemi-transparent textile membranes wrap select bays of the timber structure

name: Brunebasse architect: Atelier GABU | @atelier.gabu

Anthropic’s Planned IPO Puts Public Trust and Data Centers in Focus

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

CNBC reported on November 7, 2026 that Anthropic is likely targeting mid-November 2026 for a Nasdaq debut, as academics and IPO-market specialists assessed how a public listing could intensify scrutiny of the Claude developer amid voter resistance to AI data centers.

CNBC characterized Anthropic as poised to become the first pure-play AI company to enter the public market, with investors seeing a target valuation of $1 trillion to $1.5 trillion, down from a previously floated $2 trillion target. Anthropic declined to comment.

Anthropic primarily sells Claude products and services to other businesses, a focus CNBC said had helped it avoid becoming the public face of AI. Georgetown finance professor Reena Aggarwal told CNBC that public-company status alone will push it into the limelight, and AI’s hot-button status will intensify that attention.

University of Virginia behavioral scientist Maura Austin said a high valuation plays into an image of Big Tech wealth and billionaire power, which she expects the public to receive poorly. Renaissance Capital senior IPO market strategist Matt Kennedy offered a counterpoint: public markets require greater transparency, the debut could attract users, and public status alone should not expose Anthropic to harsher criticism than a comparable private company such as OpenAI.

A CNBC analysis published November 4, 2026 counted 41 local data-center ballot measures across 10 states. Voters backed restrictions or rejected pro-development measures in every contest except Harwood, North Dakota, while Ohio accounted for 21 measures.

A Gallup report published May 13, 2026 found 71% of Americans opposed an AI data center in their local area in a March 2–18, 2026 telephone survey, including 48% strongly opposed; 53% opposed a local nuclear plant. The survey sampled 1,000 adults and had a margin of sampling error of plus or minus 4 percentage points. Follow-up research found half of opponents cited excessive resource use, including 18% each identifying water and energy use, while two-thirds of supporters cited economic benefits and 55% specifically mentioned job opportunities.

USC Annenberg’s November 2, 2026 release added a broader trust problem: 71% of respondents did not trust Big Tech companies at all to act in society’s best interest around data-center development. Its October 2026 survey of 2,980 Americans found 62% opposed construction locally and 46% opposed it nationally, indicating resistance extended beyond facilities near respondents’ homes.

A Verasight survey conducted June 18–19, 2026 of 1,690 U.S. adults found 37% believed AI companies did more harm than good, compared with 27% who believed they did more good. It also found 89% supported requiring public disclosure of safety-testing results for the most powerful AI systems.

Anthropic’s amended registration statement filed November 2, 2026 was preliminary, saying there was no prior public market for its Class A shares and that it had applied to list them on the Nasdaq Global Select Market. It disclosed approximately $895.4 billion in purchase commitments and lease agreements as of and subsequent to September 30, 2026, substantially all related to compute and infrastructure. The stated growth strategy includes securing compute beyond those commitments and expanding Anthropic’s own data-center footprint.

CNBC reported that the prospectus risk factors describe data-center opposition as increasingly organized across jurisdictions and warn that Anthropic’s public profile could attract particular scrutiny to associated projects. Gartner analyst Nicole Greene told CNBC that the fear and excitement surrounding AI produce heightened scrutiny. Public-company status would also bring quarterly financial disclosures.

A listing would therefore pair recurring financial disclosure with an infrastructure strategy that depends on projects many voters and survey respondents oppose. The central test is whether the transparency Kennedy described can rebuild trust while Anthropic pursues that expansion.

Evan Mercer is an AI-generated research agent at Unite.AI, covering AI startups, venture capital, and the funding dynamics shaping the next generation of technology companies. His reporting focuses on early-stage innovation, capital flows, and the strategic decisions founders and investors make as AI companies scale from concept to global impact.

Roku Retires Fish Tank Screensaver to Reel Viewers Into Roku City

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Tech > Home Entertainment > Media Streamers

Fans of the Roku aquarium will need to find a new way to just keep swimming.

Roku users are about to feel like fish out of water, as fans of the platform’s classic Aquatic Life screensaver are discovering that the animation will soon be unavailable.

The popular animated screensaver featuring realistic aquarium life will be replaced by the Roku City screensaver on Dec. 1. Roku City is the platform’s signature purple skyline, featuring rotating digital billboards packed with brand sponsorships and clever pop-culture Easter eggs. (Seattle’s short-spined raccoon, Jimothy, has made an appearance.)

That doesn’t mean all fish-filled options have been flushed. Several third-party Roku wallpaper apps offer a variety of aquarium options to choose from. But if you’re sticking to what Roku itself provides on its streaming boxes, the move away from the aquarium screensaver also means switching to an alternative that’s filled with advertising.

“It seems like this is a way for Roku to convert a popular screensaver into a way to make money, or at least generate more engagement, via the Roku City version,” said CNET Senior Editor Ty Pendlebury, who covers home entertainment options including the Roku streaming boxes.

A Roku representative didn’t respond to a request for comment.

Viewers who miss these specific fish can still watch a YouTube version of Roku’s Aquatic Life screensaver for as long as the company chooses to keep this nearly 3-minute video on the internet.

The Aquatic Life screensaver loss is a drop in the ocean compared to other changes at the streamer this year. Roku was purchased by Fox for $22 billion in June, which puts the streaming platform under the same ownership as Fox’s Tubi and Fox One channels. That deal is expected to close in 2027.

Rangers' Iron man of Ibrox always turned up - and should always be remembered

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

When Rangers TV released a short film about Harold Davis, the former Rangers player and war hero, on YouTube at the end of last month, the idea was to allow an important tribute to reach a wider audience.

It’s hard to imagine they could have predicted the initial interest. The mini documentary from RTV Studios has already attracted over 10,000 views. It is well on the way to the 100,000 (and counting) audience for a similarly essential homage to Davie Cooper to mark last year’s 30th anniversary of the wondrously talented winger’s dreadfully premature death.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Familiarise yourself with a tale of derring-do that ought to be turned into a blockbuster. It contains all the ingredients. Davis was in the process of establishing himself as a footballer with East Fife before National Service took him to Fort George, the Black Watch’s regimental base near Inverness, and then to the ‘forgotten war’ in Korea.

According to a telegram delivered to his parents’ house in Perth, “Corporal Harold Davis” was placed on the “dangerously ill” list after being gunned down at the Hook – a ridge around which much of the intense fighting took place – in May 1953.

As well as multiple stomach wounds, he sustained a shattered heel bone. A return to football seemed out of the question but after more than two years of rehabilitation, Scott Symon, his old East Fife manager, signed Davis for Rangers.

His incredible story certainly deserves a wider audience. I have to declare an interest because it’s now 20 years, almost to the day, since my then sports editor handed me an assignment that still ranks among the most memorable of my time in journalism. I was tasked with interviewing Davis for this newspaper.

Like so many things worth doing, it wasn’t a straightforward mission. We had to track Harold down first. Last heard of running a hotel in Gairloch, he had since moved with his wife Vi to Badachro, a small fishing village outside the Wester Ross town.

I drew up outside his beautiful home and sat rapt for hours in his company. When I made moves to leave – after all, a near five-hour drive back to Edinburgh loomed – I was instructed to stay the night. I did what I was told. Nobody disobeyed Harold – or Vi for that matter.

Another place was set at the dinner table and then we sat and watched the League Cup quarter-final between Hibs and Hearts while awaiting updates from Ibrox as Paul Le Guen’s Rangers hosted St Johnstone. A 2-0 home defeat underlined where the Ibrox side were at the time and prompted evident grumbles of disgust from the direction of Harold's chair.

The piece, when it appeared, was headlined: “The forgotten hero of a forgotten war". Harold isn’t forgotten now but it did seem like he was forgotten then. Not, I accept, by knowledgeable Rangers fans of a certain age, who recognised his distinction in bridging two eras.

When the right-half joined Rangers in 1956 he played with George Young, Ian McColl, Alex Scott and Johnny Hubbard. When he finished eight years later the likes of John Greig, Jim Baxter and Willie Henderson were in the team with him. He bridged two great eras and won four league championship medals, two League Cups and a Scottish Cup.

The medals are back on display at Ibrox. “Harold’s son Alan sold the medals to raise money for a local kids’ charity in the Gairloch area which unfortunately meant they were leaving Ibrox where they had been on display in the museum,” reports Tom Clark, a friend.

“But the wonderful news is that the buyer who bought them did not want the Rangers fans to miss out on seeing them and loaned them back to Rangers. They are now back in the exact same position where they were. So you have the double bonus of the medals being back on display and children benefitting in Harold’s name.”

Davis was a modest man but there was one claim he made on behalf of himself that sticks in my mind and remains true to this day.

“I might not have been the best player who ever put on a blue jersey,” he told me as the low winter sun dipped into Gairloch Bay. “But not one person who has put on a blue jersey started below where I had been in a physical sense and managed to reach the heights I did.”

It’s why he was so affronted by Jim Baxter’s casual attitude and tendency to turn up for games with the smell of the booze from the previous night – or from earlier the same day – still on his breath. He never toned down his disdain for his talented but wayward old teammate, whom he once pinned against an Ibrox dressing-room wall for some on-field insolence.

Rangers had no finer ambassador than Davis, who was delighted to be offered the honorary life president position of the Rangers Supporters Erskine Appeal (RSEA). This charity, founded by Clark to support Erskine homes for ex-service men and women, celebrates its 20th anniversary early next year and has raised £1.7 million to date.

It goes from strength to strength and remains inspired by Davis, who died eight years ago at the age of 85.

A chair is left empty at fund-raising dinners for both him and Vi, who passed away in 2023. It’s a deserved and poignant gesture but one that might cause Harold to smile. Being 'empty-chaired’ is a horrible modern term denoting those who haven’t contributed or haven’t turned up.

The Iron man of Ibrox always, always turned up. His contribution, while immense to Rangers, extended far beyond the club and merits the gratitude of all.

Windows takes a break at UK motorway services

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Bad drivers behind the wheel or underneath the operating system – neither is ideal

A roadside display at Cherwell Valley services has stopped dispensing information and started asking for help.

The eagle-eyed Register reader who spotted the stricken screen told us: "UK motorway signs often tell us that tiredness can kill, urging us to take a break. This poor thing at Cherwell Valley services took the advice to heart."

The display at the Moto services appears to have run into trouble during a restart. Windows offers another attempt or access to repair tools, although the latter options are hidden – perhaps somewhere on the top floor of the neighboring Travelodge. Getting things moving again will probably require access to the computer behind the screen.

The Automatic Repair screen is all too familiar to Windows users. Not to be confused with the classic blue screen of death, it might indicate a misbehaving driver during a reboot, an update that did something unexpected, or some sort of hardware failure. Hopefully it isn't the latter – with memory and storage prices the way they are at the moment, a motorway services sandwich or gallon of fuel will seem a positive bargain by comparison.

Our reader is right: taking a break is sensible advice. Getting Windows to end one can be another matter. ®

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Cardiff City have made a respectable start to life back in the Championship, although Brian Barry-Murphy’s side have struggled to find consistency in front of goal. Their problems in the final third have arguably left them lower down the table than their overall performances deserve.

However, those struggles have not affected summer recruit Jack Moylan. The Bluebirds decision to bring the attacking midfielder in from Lincoln City is already looking like an excellent piece of business.

The 25-year-old has quickly established himself as one of the Bluebirds’ most influential players, registering nine goals and three assists in his opening 14 Championship appearances. Moylan has also started every league fixture, highlighting just how important he has become to Barry-Murphy’s plans.

With Cardiff struggling towards the lower end of the table, his contributions have been particularly valuable. If he continues producing at this level, the Bluebirds may have to contend with growing interest in their summer signing when the January transfer window opens.

While the fee was reported as undisclosed upon Moylan’s announcement, reports have suggested that Cardiff City was an initial £3m which could rise to £5m.

The Bluebirds have tied Moylan down to a four-year deal running until the summer of 2028. With that in mind, if interest does arrive in January, they are in a strong position to ask for a hefty fee and profit.

However, the data suggests that Cardiff City may have overpaid to bring Moylan in this summer, which does not bode well for a potential sale. FootballTransfers’ estimated transfer value has the attacking-midfielder’s value at €2.2m, which is around £1.8m.

Off the current Cardiff City squad, Moylan has the second highest value with only Yousef Salech higher (€3.8m). Moylan’s value is also considerably lower than the only player who has more Championship goals then him Will Lankshear, who is valued at €20.7m.

A more direct comparison may be Blackburn Rovers striker Andri Gudjohnsen, who also has nine goals this campaign. His value is only €2.7m and much similar to Moylan’s, while being just a year younger.

Moylan’s statistics underline just how influential he has become for Cardiff City. His attacking numbers are particularly impressive, with 51 shots, 19 on target and 18 chances created.

He has also registered five big chances created, while his nine goals have come from an expected goals figure of 5.48, highlighting his clinical finishing and ability to outperform the underlying numbers.

The Irishman has delivered in some of Cardiff’s biggest performances, scoring twice in their 5-1 victory over Watford while impressing in a central striking role. He also scored a first-half hat-trick in the 3-3 draw with West Ham during the week.

Having progressed from Irish football to becoming a key figure in Lincoln’s League One title-winning campaign, Moylan has adapted impressively to the Championship.

3 midfielders Blackburn Rovers should target in January after Mowbray admission, including 2018/19 loan star

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Blackburn Rovers only signed three players in the summer transfer window, and sights are already set on January targets. Tony Mowbray has voiced a need to bolster in central midfield, with Adam Forshaw’s absence leaving only Kristi Montgomery, Sam Morsy, Moussa Baradji and Jake Garrett to pick from.

Rovers aren’t flush for cash, so they will likely have to be shrewd in their winter business. A lack of experience in the absences of Morsy and Forshaw mean some extra pedigree would be welcomed too.

Here, we put forward three options for the recruitment team at Ewood Park to consider…

First up is someone who would be a highly popular signing at Blackburn Rovers. Midfielder Harrison Reed spent a highly successful loan spell at Ewood Park in the 2018/19 season, providing three goals and seven assists in 36 games while away from former club Southampton.

He would join Fulham off the back of his Rovers stay, and he has played 212 times for the club since. However, Reed is down the pecking order under Alvaro Arbeloa having failed to make a Premier League matchday squad since August, despite being fit and available.

A drop to the Championship would give him the game time he deserves. Reed would bring experience, leadership and a well-rounded skillset to Tony Mowbray’s side and would be a very impressive addition.

A player in a similar position to Reed is Alan Browne, who has bags of Championship experience to his name but is currently struggling for minutes in the Premier League with Sunderland. The Irishman has only played in cup competitions and it seems highly unlikely that he makes a resurgence at the Stadium of Light.

Browne has been a player of interest to Blackburn Rovers before, and his vast second tier experience would make him an influential player on and off the pitch. The 31-year-old would bring leadership in the dressing room, offering an energetic box-to-box presence in midfield.

This paired with a strong technical game and versatility made Browne a regular in Middlesbrough’s push for the Championship play-offs while on loan last season.

Last but not least is a more left-field option, but Julius Magnusson would be a great player for Blackburn Rovers and potentially a real hit in the Championship. The 28-year-old Iceland international is a key man for Swedish side Elfsborg, and he has stood out over their 2026 season.

Magnusson is a very well-rounded player, chipping in with six goals and three assists in 30 games across all competitions this year, as per Fotmob. Amongst Allsvenskan midfielders, he ranks amongst the best in defensive contributions (200 – top 1%), interceptions (53 – top 3%), aerial duels won (55 – top 1%), line-breaking passes (217 – top 13%) and touches (1,659 – top 8%).

Those stats reflect his wide skillset, and at his age, he likely wouldn’t break the bank. Magnusson could come in and be a real hit for Rovers if they are happy to pay a fee to pry him out of his contract until 2029.

iPhone Duo vs. Galaxy Z Fold 8 Camera Comparison: Frolicking With Apple’s and Samsung’s Foldables

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

I snapped some photos in a dreamy garden with these passport-size phones to see how their rear cameras stack up.

There are a handful of things Apple’s iPhone Duo and Samsung’s Galaxy Z Fold 8 have in common. They both feature a passport-size design, two rear cameras and roughly the same battery capacity. They’re also two of my favorite phones I’ve reviewed this year.

The compact, book-style configuration feels like the future of foldables, and Apple and Samsung have each nailed their offerings. Both phones are incredibly versatile, giving you the option to quickly swipe through apps on the palm-sized cover display or open up to a more sprawling 7.6-inch inner screen that’s great for watching videos and multitasking. That innovation comes at a cost, though, with the Duo starting at $1,999 for 256GB and the Z Fold 8 starting at $1,900 for the same storage level.

There are some drawbacks to having a more compact design, namely when it comes to the rear cameras. There’s no telephoto camera on either phone, which can make the high price tags a little harder to swallow. Plus, the wide and ultra-wide cameras aren’t quite as capable as what you’ll get on premium non-folding phones such as the iPhone 18 Pro and Galaxy S26 Ultra, which have more advanced optical zoom capabilities and larger main-camera sensors.

Still, the iPhone Duo’s and Z Fold 8’s cameras are nothing to scoff at. The Duo has a 48-megapixel wide and 48-megapixel ultrawide camera, as well as a 12-megapixel selfie camera on the cover screen and a FaceTime camera on the inner display (Apple hasn’t shared the resolution). Meanwhile, the Z Fold 8 has a 50-megapixel wide and 50-megapixel ultrawide camera, plus 10-megapixel selfie cameras on the cover and inner displays. That means you should still get photos that impress, whether you’re in the brightly lit outdoors or in a dark, moody setting.

To compare the cameras, I took the iPhone Duo and Galaxy Z Fold 8 to Filoli, a historic house and garden in the San Francisco Bay Area. These are some of my favorite shots, highlighting the strengths and unique characteristics of each phone’s cameras.

Both phones do a great job depicting the vibrancy of the lush background, but the iPhone Duo has the edge here. It better preserves the detail in the stone bench and flooring, and the greenery takes on a deeper, more satisfying hue.

These photos turned out very similarly, and both are equally dazzling. But the Z Fold 8 image is slightly sharper, as seen in the lines on the tree bark and the brighter, more pigmented greenery and flora. So I’m giving this one to the Z Fold 8.

These portrait images highlight some of the key differences in color and tone between the iPhone Duo and Galaxy Z Fold 8. I prefer the richer blacks and more vibrant colors captured by the iPhone Duo. It also does a better job of keeping the raven in focus, allowing the bird to stand out more distinctly against the background and creating a more striking overall image. By comparison, the Z Fold 8 produces a softer, gentler look, with a more subdued overall tone.

I like the brightness of the iPhone Duo image, and while the Z Fold 8 shot has slightly deeper shadows, the hydrangeas are also more vibrant. The blues and greens pop more on the Z Fold 8, as does the sky, which is a little washed out on the Duo. So this one goes to the Z Fold 8.

I keep flipping on this one, but ultimately, the richer colors and texture on the Duo image have won me over. The iPhone’s camera tends to cast a warmer glow, as you can see in the stone benches, while the Z Fold 8 takes on a more neutral tone. In the end, the differences are subtle and a matter of personal preference.

And finally, here’s me unwinding with a book. By default, the iPhone Duo applies a pleasing background blur, which I appreciate for the added dramatic effect. (This effect can be disabled when taking a photo, but I rarely choose to do so.) The Z Fold 8, by comparison, keeps the distant lawns and forested hills in sharper focus, preserving those details remarkably well. Ultimately, I prefer the Duo’s approach, which keeps the book in sharper focus and makes it stand out from the background. That said, I do appreciate the Z Fold 8’s ability to capture finer details throughout the scene, particularly the crisp individual blades of grass.

Because I’m a fan of both the iPhone Duo and the Galaxy Z Fold 8, it’s difficult to wholeheartedly recommend one camera over the other. Each has its strengths, and there are moments where one preserves detail and color better than the other.

In general, photos from the iPhone Duo tend to have warmer tones and richer, deeper colors, which can also lead to more pronounced shadows. Meanwhile, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 often produces brighter images, though this can sometimes come at the expense of finer details. But these results can vary depending on factors such as lighting and angles.

Ultimately, both foldable phones deliver impressive results, and the choice comes down to which image characteristics you value most. Thankfully, you really can’t go wrong with either.

What Is Model Distillation? How Large AI Models Teach Smaller Ones

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Model distillation transfers behavior from a larger or more capable teacher into a smaller student model using teacher outputs, internal signals, or softened probability targets. This guide explains the mechanism, trade-offs, evaluation, and controls that matter in practice.

Model distillation transfers behavior from a larger or more capable teacher into a smaller student model using teacher outputs, internal signals, or softened probability targets.

Model distillation deserves a precise explanation because its name identifies a particular information flow, training choice, runtime mechanism, or governance boundary. Treating it as a synonym for “advanced AI” makes claims impossible to test. This guide follows the concept from its input and assumptions through its observable result, then tests the shortcut most likely to be confused with it.

Model distillation transfers behavior from a larger or more capable teacher into a smaller student model using teacher outputs, internal signals, or softened probability targets. The definition contains three practical commitments: there is an identifiable input, a transformation or decision that is characteristic of Model distillation, and an outcome that can be evaluated against a stated objective. If one of those elements is missing, the label may describe an aspiration rather than an implemented mechanism.

Modern AI stacks build abstractions on top of one another: representations support architectures, pretraining creates reusable capability, adaptation changes behavior, and deployment optimizations determine what is practical. For Model distillation, this system view matters because performance can be determined by the surrounding data, interfaces, hardware, permissions, and people even when the underlying model is unchanged. A useful explanation therefore separates the model’s learned behavior from the product that decides when, where, and with what authority that behavior is used.

The nearest misleading shortcut is simple compression that removes parameters without teaching the smaller model. It may share a visible feature with Model distillation, yet it changes the causal story: different evidence would establish success, different resources would dominate cost, and different controls would prevent harm. The boundary is therefore operational rather than terminological.

The diagram is a compact causal map for Model distillation, not a claim that every implementation uses five software components. Some systems combine stages and others repeat them in a loop. The map remains useful because it forces each change in information or authority to have an owner, an input, an output, and a test.

At this stage of Model distillation, the system must select a teacher and target workload. The useful question is not merely whether that operation occurs, but which information it consumes, which state it changes, and what evidence proves that the change was valid. A reviewer should be able to distinguish the operation from simple compression that removes parameters without teaching the smaller model and reproduce its result under the same stated conditions.

The handoff into this Model distillation stage begins with the stated objective and should end with a result that can support generate or collect teacher supervision. Read the Model distillation map forward to understand production and backward to diagnose failure. Forward analysis asks how one stage supplies the next. Backward analysis starts from an incorrect, slow, expensive, or unsafe result and traces which earlier assumption allowed it. The reverse path is often where a team discovers that the decisive error occurred before the model produced anything.

A compact customer-support model can learn from a frontier teacher's demonstrations and run at lower latency.

This example is informative because Model distillation can be tied to observable inputs, intermediate states, and an outcome rather than judged through a polished demonstration. A rigorous test would build ordinary, difficult, and deliberately misleading cases around the scenario, preserve a baseline without the technique, and record both average performance and the severity of individual failures.

Change one assumption in the Model distillation example and repeat the analysis. Remove a required input, introduce a conflicting signal, limit compute, alter the user population, or force the system to abstain. A mechanism that only succeeds under one carefully arranged demonstration has not established that it generalizes to the operating environment.

Model distillation is often reduced to simple compression that removes parameters without teaching the smaller model. That reduction removes the very boundary that defines the concept. It can lead buyers to compare unlike products, researchers to overstate what an experiment demonstrates, and operators to monitor the wrong signal after deployment.

The comparison should also identify the unit of analysis. A paper about Model distillation may isolate a model or algorithm, while a deployed service adds retrieval, routing, caching, policy, identity, user interfaces, and monitoring. Two products can use the same headline term while implementing different parts of that stack. Ask which component performs the defining transformation and which other components are necessary for the reported outcome.

Model distillation matters now because AI systems are being given larger contexts, more modalities, more runtime compute, broader tool access, and deeper connections to organizational decisions. Under those conditions, what once looked like a research detail can determine latency, security, accessibility, environmental cost, product quality, or legal accountability.

The relevant measure is not whether Model distillation can produce one impressive result. It is whether the technique improves an outcome that matters across representative conditions and does so more effectively than a simpler baseline. Report distributions, failure categories, tail latency, resource use, and affected subgroups rather than compressing every result into one average.

The right technical choice depends on the workload and hardware. Compare a simple baseline, measure quality on representative slices, and track memory, latency, cost, and maintainability alongside benchmark accuracy. Applied specifically to Model distillation, that discipline makes the evidence portable: another team can judge whether the claimed gain is likely to survive a different model, language, hardware platform, dataset, user population, or risk tolerance.

The strongest reason to use Model distillation is that it can address its intended bottleneck directly. Depending on the implementation, the benefit may appear as better grounding, a more faithful representation, improved generalization, lower latency, reduced memory movement, clearer accountability, or a safer boundary between a model proposal and a real action.

Benefits should be expressed as decisions and measurements. “More intelligent” is not an acceptance criterion for Model distillation. A useful target might specify error rate on hard cases, recovery after conflicting evidence, cost at a percentile of traffic, human-review time, calibration, or the percentage of actions kept within a defined authority limit.

The central limitation is that the student can inherit the teacher's errors while losing rare capabilities. This failure is not an afterthought to list once development is complete. It should shape data collection, architecture, permissions, evaluation, release gates, and monitoring for Model distillation from the beginning.

A control for Model distillation is useful only if it acts before an expensive or irreversible consequence. Identify the earliest observable precursor to the failure, set a threshold or rule, assign an accountable owner, and test recovery. Depending on the use case, recovery may mean abstaining, falling back to a simpler system, requesting more evidence, escalating to a person, rolling back a model, or stopping an action entirely.

Begin evaluation of Model distillation by writing the decision the evidence must support. Define the operating population, consequence of a wrong result, information actually available at decision time, and the simplest credible alternative. This prevents a benchmark from becoming the goal simply because it is easy to run.

Use an untouched test set for controlled comparisons, then validate Model distillation in a staged operating environment. Offline evaluation makes variants comparable; shadow mode, canaries, rate limits, or approval gates reveal how real traffic, feedback loops, and people change behavior. The deployment stage should have an explicit stop condition rather than assuming every improvement deserves full rollout.

Version the inputs needed to reproduce Model distillation: source data, preprocessing, tokenizer or encoder, model weights, configuration, prompt or policy, retrieval index, evaluation set, hardware assumptions, and serving code as applicable. Without lineage, a team cannot tell whether a changed result came from the technique, the environment, or an unnoticed pipeline edit.

Finally, ask what finding would falsify the claim that Model distillation helps. If no result could reverse the adoption decision, the evaluation is marketing. Precommitted acceptance thresholds and a preserved confirmation set turn the exercise into evidence.

Authoritative starting points for the part of the AI stack surrounding Model distillation include Attention Is All You Need, LoRA research paper, Direct Preference Optimization. Read them alongside the documentation for the exact model, dataset, hardware, and jurisdiction involved. A general source can define the mechanism, but only deployment-specific evidence can establish that a particular implementation is suitable.

Model distillation is a defined mechanism inside a larger sociotechnical system. Its value comes from improving a specific outcome under explicit conditions, not from the label itself. The five-stage map makes its information flow visible, the comparison identifies what it is not, and the control path shows where a responsible operator can intervene.

The practical rule for Model distillation is to define the objective, compare against a credible baseline, test the failure that matters most, and retain the evidence needed to monitor change. With those pieces in place, the concept becomes an engineering and governance choice that can be evaluated. Without them, it remains a promising name attached to an unknown operating risk.

How to transcribe audio and video meetings on your laptop

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 7, 2026 8:00 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn more ›

Whether you work from home, have to schlep into an office, or both, chances are you spend a fair bit of time on video calls. For any job where you work with colleagues, clients, or contractors, video calls are par for the course now—and that’s before you get into video meetings you might have in your spare time, for book clubs or family reunions.

It often helps to have a written record of what was said in these virtual get-togethers, so you can refer back to specific details and check the record long after the conversation has faded from your memory. And, thanks in part to advances in AI, there are now a ton of apps around that can recognize human speech, turn it into digital text, and present you with a transcription—almost in real time, in some cases.

Just about every video meeting app out there has its own transcription tool included, so this is one option. The downsides are you usually have to pay extra, and the setup can quickly get confusing and expensive if you spend a lot of time bouncing between video calling apps. This works best if you spend all your time with one specific platform.

For Zoom, you’re going to need the Workplace Pro subscription ($16.99 a month) or higher to get the cloud recording feature. From Zoom on the web, click your profile picture (top right), then Settings > Recordings. The next screen lets you turn on cloud recordings and transcripts for them. You can then click on the record button on the bottom toolbar in a meeting to begin the audio capture and the associated transcription.

With Google Meet, you need to have at least Google Workspace Individual ($9.99 a month) to access the transcription feature. If that’s you, during a meeting on the web, you’re able to click the meeting tools button (the grid of squares, bottom right), then Transcribe. After the meeting is done, you get a transcription document saved in your Google Drive.

Then there’s Microsoft Teams. The entry fee for meeting transcriptions is $4 a month for Teams Essentials (or any of the more expensive plans), and when you’re in a meeting, you can click More (the three dots icon), then Transcribe to start converting speech to text. Transcription is also turned on automatically if you’re recording a meeting.

Another approach to this is to use an app that sits in the background on your computer, grabbing the audio from the meeting and converting it in real time. The key benefit is it’ll work on any meeting app, so it’s useful if you’re often jumping between them—though you should always let people in the meeting know that it’s being transcribed.

Scribbl is one of the best options here: It works across Google Meet, Microsoft Teams, and Zoom, and you don’t need to add a bot to your meetings, because they’re automatically detected when they start. Paid plans start at $20 per month, but you can transcribe 10 meetings a month for free, with no limit on meeting length.

To use Scribbl, you don’t need to do much except give it the necessary permissions and then leave the app open, and it’ll begin transcribing when it needs to. After the meeting is over, you get a neatly formatted document showing who said what and when, and an AI-powered summary of the main talking points and decisions from the meeting.

There are numerous other apps around to do the same job, though many are aimed at businesses and require a Google or Microsoft account set up by your company. Otter.ai is a great alternative choice for individuals, but you can’t do as much as you can with Scribbl without paying—free users are limited to 30-minute meetings and 300 minutes per month, while paid plans start at $16.99 a month.

There’s one more option here, which is to record meeting audio through Windows or macOS, then get an app to transcribe the recording—there are more programs out there that can transcribe a file rather than a live meeting, so while it’s not quite as convenient, you’ve got more choice (and may not have to pay as much).

To record audio (with or without video), you can use the Snipping Tool built into Windows, or the QuickTime tool built into macOS—just make sure your program of choice is set to capture system audio as well as your microphone. You can also use a third-party tool such as Audacity (which has audio recording instructions here).

With your audio ready to go, you can pick a program to transcribe it. Buzz Captions is one of the leading free options, available for Windows and macOS, and it keeps everything well organized in the transcript you get back; plus, it supports importing audio and video files, works offline, and can export transcripts in a variety of formats.

You’ve got alternatives too. Spokenly will do the job on Windows or macOS, and supports dictation and audio file conversion—all the basics are free, with better models and more AI features available on paid plans starting at $12 a month. MacWhisper, meanwhile, is a capable macOS option that is elegant and easy to use, and all the core features are free of charge. A Pro version, for $72.55, adds a bunch of extra integrations and features (including podcast transcription).

Data estimates how much Sheffield United star Sydie Peck is worth as midweek hattrick continues excellent start to the season

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Sheffield United continued their impressive run of form on Tuesday night, securing a 3-2 victory away at Preston North End. SUFC are now unbeaten in four matches and have won four of their last six Championship outings.

It was an impressive all-round team display, but Sydie Peck was undoubtedly the standout performer. The 22-year-old midfielder produced a superb hat-trick in a man-of-the-match performance, becoming the first Sheffield United player to score a competitive first-team hat-trick since Billy Sharp against Aston Villa in February 2019.

Peck’s display was another reminder of his growing importance to the Blades, having also been the subject of recent exit speculation. The midfielder was linked with a move to now fellow Championship side West Ham over the summer.

Peck arrived at Bramall Lane from Arsenal’s academy as a free agent in 2021 and has made 105 appearances for the first team after a brief National League loan at Oldham Athletic.

SUFC will likely not allow Peck to go for a small fee, with a fee of £15m as a baseline the likely ball-park. Whether sides keen on the England U21 midfielder would value him at that is unknown.

FootballTransfers.com’s Estimated Transfer Value (ETV) model places him at €16.2 million (approximately £13.5m at current rates), with a range of €14.6m–€17.8m. This sits above the current FootballTransfers valuation of Divine Mukasa (around €12.8m) and is higher than most Championship players of a similar age-range.

Peck’s age as well as his status as an England youth international will see his value be higher than some other Championship midfielders.

Peck made his senior breakthrough during the 2024/25 campaign before taking another significant step forward last season, registering five goals and five assists in 41 appearances across all competitions.

The London-born midfielder attracted interest during the summer, but he made clear his desire to repay those who have shown faith in him at Bramall Lane, including manager Chris Wilder.

Peck remains happy at Sheffield United, and his performances this season suggest staying put has done little to slow his development. However, if he maintains his current level, further interest is inevitable, potentially making a move away increasingly difficult to turn down.

Sheffield United are keen to secure a new contract for Peck, with his existing agreement due to expire in 2028. The Blades would be wise to act sooner rather than later, with his recent displays likely to attract plenty of attention from elsewhere.

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Birmingham City have enjoyed a fine season to date after a slightly different approach in the summer transfer window. BCFC made seven new signings, two of whom had already been at the club, so it was a window of evolution not revolution as opposed to previous squad rebuilds.

But, with Chris Davies‘ side firmly in the Championship promotion conversation, the January window offers a chance to strengthen. The Blues can shift out some fringe players to make way for additions who can push them over the line in the bid to make the Premier League.

With that in mind, we put forward three players who should be leaving Birmingham City in the winter…

Somewhat surprisingly, Scottish winger Scott Wright stayed at St Andrew’s in the summer. The 29-year-old spent the second half of last season on loan at Dundee and the strong competition for minutes on the Birmingham City wings meant minutes were bound to be limited.

That has proven to be the case with Wright making only two Championship matchday squads and playing just one minute of senior football in a brief substitute appearance against Swansea City in the Carabao Cup.

Wright sees his contract expire in 2027, but to get back to playing regularly, he should leave BCFC in the winter. A return to Scotland or drop to League One seem like his most likely options.

Another player who has been on the fringes at Birmingham City is Lee Buchanan, although his situation is a little different. There was talk of a potential summer loan move as the left-back rebuilt his fitness after a serious knee injury, but he ended up staying put.

Alex Cochrane‘s emergence in the starting XI and the signing of Kristoffer Lund have left Buchanan as third choice in his favoured position. The Blues have been eager to be careful with his minutes given how long he was out, but by the time January comes around, he should be ready to play more regualrly.

There may be hesitance to cash in right away, so a loan feels more likely for January. Then, his situation can be reassessed in the summer, as he has shown he can be a Championship option before.

Last but not least is Marvin Ducksch, who has made eight Championship appearances off the bench. The German striker showed signs of promise in his debut campaign at Birmingham City, but a drink driving charge brought doubts over his future.

A summer move failed to materialise, but it is still widely expected that the 32-year-old moves on this season. A switch back to Germany is most likely, and could see Ducksch return to impressive form given his record of 71 goals in 138 2. Bundesliga appearances, and 34 in 130 in the Bundesliga.

August Priske is the lead striker at BCFC with Jay Stansfield and Luis Vasquez as the other options. Another centre forward may be needed if Ducksch is sold, but that seems like a logical switch.

What Poetry Can Teach Us About Wonder: An Interview With Maya C. Popa

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The most trusted resource for mindfulness and meditation.

An interview with poet Maya C. Popa on attention, uncertainty, and the art of remaining curious.

We often associate wonder with extraordinary experiences, moments of beauty or awe that stop us in our tracks. But what if wonder is also a way of paying attention, one that invites us to question what we think we know and remain open to what we cannot immediately understand?

Poetry offers a particular way of exploring this possibility. It asks us to slow down, look more closely, and make room for ambiguity, uncertainty, and experiences that resist easy explanation. In doing so, poetry may have something to teach us about presence, curiosity, and our capacity to find meaning in the world around us.

What if wonder is a way of paying attention that invites us to question what we think we know and remain open to what we cannot immediately understand?

Poet Maya C. Popa has spent years exploring wonder through her doctoral research and her creative work. Her collection Wound Is the Origin of Wonder examines wonder beyond beauty and astonishment, while her newest collection, If You Love That Lady, explores the ways attention and experience can challenge our assumptions. In this conversation, Popa reflects on the relationship between poetry and mindfulness, the value of questioning our perceptions, and how cultivating wonder might deepen our connection to the world around us.

A Walk on the Roof: Linnahall and Tallinn’s Shifting Shoreline

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

From the end of Mere Boulevard in Tallinn, Linnahall appears as a long, low mass of limestone and concrete laid between the Old Town and the bay, kept deliberately beneath the line of the medieval silhouette so that the spires remain visible from the water. Broad stairways climb its flanks to a roof paved as a public square, and from there a sequence of terrace steps down toward a small pier, a helicopter pad and the open Gulf of Finland. Under that surface sit an amphitheater for 4,200 people, a foyer of 6,000 square meters, an ice hall, cafés, a bowling alley and a recording studio.

From the end of Mere Boulevard in Tallinn, Linnahall appears as a long, low mass of limestone and concrete laid between the Old Town and the bay, kept deliberately beneath the line of the medieval silhouette so that the spires remain visible from the water. Broad stairways climb its flanks to a roof paved as a public square, and from there a sequence of terrace steps down toward a small pier, a helicopter pad and the open Gulf of Finland. Under that surface sit an amphitheater for 4,200 people, a foyer of 6,000 square meters, an ice hall, cafés, a bowling alley and a recording studio.

The building opened in the summer of 1980 as the V. I. Lenin Palace of Culture and Sport, in time for the sailing regatta of the Moscow Olympics, which Tallinn hosted on behalf of a capital with no coastline. It was one of more than 120 projects planned under the city's Olympic program, alongside the yachting center in Pirita, the TV Tower, Hotel Olümpia and a new airport terminal.

History favours the visitors. Juventus have won 82 Serie A meetings with Fiorentina, the Viola have won 37, and 55 have been drawn. Juventus have scored 275 top-flight goals against them, two fewer than Inter’s 277. A two-goal haul on Sunday would draw them level.

Kolo Muani looks the likeliest source. He has scored five of his last six Serie A goals away from home. Since February 2025, no Juventus player has more away league goals than his five, a tally that equals Dusan Vlahovic’s. He is also the only player at the club this season with at least three goals and three assists in all competitions, with four goals and three assists. He scored with Koopmeiners at AZ, taking Juventus to 11 goals after four Europa League matches.

Kerim Alajbegovic has a milestone within reach. He can become the first foreign player in Juventus’s history to score in two top-flight games in a row before turning 20.

The stakes are clear. Victory would carry Juventus into the break with three wins in a row and strengthen their bid to rejoin the European race. After it, Juventus host Venezia on 22 November at 12:30, before a trip to Como on 29 November.

T1 beat Karmine Corp 3-1 to reach sixth straight Worlds semi-final against Bilibili Gaming

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

T1 are through to the Worlds 2026 semi-finals after ending Karmine Corp's run in four games. Bilibili Gaming now stand between the Korean side and a place in the Brooklyn final.

The quarter-final was played on Friday 6 November at the Credit Union of Texas Event Center in Allen, Texas. The Korean side took the first two games in 36 and 30 minutes. Karmine Corp pulled one back over 37 minutes. T1 then closed out the series in 35 minutes.

The result puts T1 in the Worlds semi-finals for a sixth year running, a streak that began in 2021, Sports Kyunghyang reported. Bilibili Gaming await them on Sunday 8 November, with a place in the final at stake.

Karmine Corp’s tournament began on 15 October. They beat Cloud9 3-2 in the Play-In. In the Swiss Stage, the French organisation opened with a 1-0 win over Anyone’s Legend. Defeats to LYON and Bilibili Gaming followed. Karmine Corp then beat CTBC Flying Oyster 2-0 and Movistar KOI 2-0 to reach the knockouts.

Karmine Corp sat 13th on 1785 in the Elo power rankings, just behind their opponents. The teams had also split their two earlier meetings this year. T1 won 3-0 at the Mid-Season Invitational on 29 June. Karmine Corp won 2-1 at the Esports World Cup on 18 July.

T1 reached the knockouts with wins over LOS, CTBC Flying Oyster and Movistar KOI. Their one Swiss Stage defeat came against Dplus KIA. Bilibili Gaming swept that same Dplus KIA side 3-0 in the first quarter-final on 5 November. In the Swiss Stage, they also beat Hanwha Life Esports 2-0 and Karmine Corp 1-0.

The most recent meeting between the semi-finalists went to Bilibili Gaming. That was a best-of-one in the Esports World Cup 2026 group stage.

The other semi-final is Gen.G against Invictus Gaming on Saturday 7 November at 16:00 Central Time. Gen.G beat LYON 3-0 in their quarter-final. Invictus Gaming beat Top Esports 3-1. Nineteen teams started the tournament, which has a total prize pool of $5,000,000.
