Benson Boone Brings Young Fan Battling Cancer Onstage at Brisbane Concert

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

"I've had the opportunity of meeting a 13-year-old girl who is battling her second round of cancer," Boone told the crowd.

Benson Boone opened the Australian leg of his Wanted Man tour with a special guest. He brought a 13-year-old fan who is battling cancer onstage to sing “Beautiful Things” with him on her birthday. Explore Benson Boone See latest videos, charts and news

The moment came at the end of Boone’s set at Brisbane Entertainment Centre on Friday (Nov. 6). The Music reported that the singer stopped to introduce the final song. Related Céline Dion Plays Air Bass at Metallica's Sphere Show Lincoln Celebrated the Art of Rejuvenation at Billboard Latin Music Week Teddy Swims' 'Mr. Know It All' Hits No. 1 on Billboard Pop Airplay Chart

“I’ve had the opportunity of meeting a 13-year-old girl who is battling her second round of cancer,” Boone told the crowd, according to the outlet. “It’s her birthday today and her birthday wish was to come on stage and sing ‘Beautiful Things.’”

The fan, Kenzie Riddle, then rose from an upper platform and joined Boone on the song. According to The Music, roughly 10,000 people were in the arena.

Riddle has Ewing’s sarcoma. As reported by Nine’s Today, she believed she had beaten the illness after about two and a half years of treatment, but the cancer returned and she is now facing intensive chemotherapy. Her family said they bought tickets to the show in July as her 13th birthday present. Her campaign to meet Boone took off online after a local musician, Fergus Hogan, shared an Instagram video about her story.

“I love his energetic style and how he’s so over the top,” Riddle told Today before the concert. “He’s kinda crazy and I love it.”

Her family also told the program: “She’s such an unbelievable inspiration to so many people… But I think even bigger than that is the community behind her.”

“Beautiful Things” was Boone’s breakout single. Released in January 2024, it reached No. 2 on the Billboard Hot 100 and became one of the most-streamed songs in the world that year. Boone followed it with his debut album Fireworks & Rollerblades that April. His second album, American Heart, arrived in 2025 and included the single “Mystical Magical.”

The Brisbane show opened Boone’s Wanted Man tour of Australia. Boone has two additional Brisbane performances scheduled for Nov. 7 and 8 before the tour heads to Melbourne for three concerts at Rod Laver Arena beginning Nov. 12. The Australian leg concludes with three shows at Sydney’s Afterpay Arena from Nov. 17-19.

This week Kengo Kuma unveiled a pyramidal brick bathhouse

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

This week on Dezeen Japanese designer Kengo Kuma’s Kai Water Culture House, which features pyramidal towers, was revealed nearing completion in Copenhagen.

The 5,800-square-metre aquatic centre on the city’s harbour will contain 10 indoor and outdoor pools and is opening next month. Kuma designed the project alongside Danish firms Cornelius Vöge, Søren Jensen and Vilhelm Lauritzen Architects.

In Sydney, museum Powerhouse Parramatta opens today. Designed by architecture studios Moreau Kusunoki and Genton, it is described as the largest cultural project in Australia since the Sydney Opera House.

Powerhouse Parramatta is located on the banks of the Parramatta River in western Sydney and wrapped by a steel-lattice exoskeleton.

Annual design week Designart Tokyo is currently taking place in venues across the Japanese capital. Among six standout projects featured by Dezeen from this year’s event, was an exhibition that explores people’s perspectives on forests and a lamp that unfolds like an accordion.

Also on show in the city was the emerging design exhibition Alter, which featured work including an AI-powered tea ceremony and washi paper furniture.

Also this week, automotive brand Fiat launched its Multiplay concept car, which can transform from an SUV into a pickup, with movable seats and an interior designed to be flexible.

Cars were also on the agenda in London, where British car brand Jaguar redesigned its flagship store in Knightsbridge. It is the only place in the world where people can see the recently unveiled Type 01 electric car.

French studio Lacaton & Vassal Architectes, which is led by architects Anne Lacaton and Jean-Philippe Vassal, was named the recipient of the Le Prix Charlotte Perriand 2027.

The studio is known for its social housing designs and was recognised by the Créateurs Design Association & Awards for contributing to contemporary architecture through its work in transforming and improving existing buildings.

Popular projects on Dezeen this week include an arched London apartment building by Alison Brooks, a former workshop converted into a Japanese home and a “quiet yet present” garden studio.

GoDaddy’s CEO won’t read AI-generated work

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

GoDaddy CEO Aman Bhutani says he won’t read a report if he suspects it was written by AI— because he knows nobody put thought into it. 

“If somebody sends me a document and I can tell pretty quickly it was written by AI, I don’t read it,” he recently told Business Insider. “If the person couldn’t take the time to write it, then I definitely shouldn’t take the time to read it.”

Bhutani says AI-generated writing is easy to identify—telltale signs include excessive length, an overly punchy tone, and language that is a little too perfect.

His goal is to make sure his employees are thinking for themselves. 

“AI only knows what the internet already knows,” he said. “It doesn’t know what we know deep inside us, and there are ideas coming from us all the time.”

Also, being able to clearly convey an idea in writing requires refining the idea itself—something Bhutani calls “the art of iteration.” 

“What I’m really looking for is: What was the original observation? What was the original hypothesis? What got you interested in this idea? Why do you think it matters?” he said. 

Bhutani is far from the only executive who still views writing as an essential workplace skill. 

Zapier CEO Wade Foster has referenced what he calls “the comprehension gap,” which happens when executives use AI to craft communications and later can’t explain their work.

Sam Altman himself has described writing as “externalized thinking,” saying putting difficult problems into words helps him clarify his ideas.

Similarly, publishing executive JD Barker has argued that writing forces people to think through weaknesses in their reasoning. 

“When someone sits down to explain a decision in plain language, they run into every soft spot in their reasoning,” Barker recently wrote in Fast Company. “A manager who can write a clear one-page memo can usually think clearly about the problem in it.”
